The \texttt{J4}, \texttt{K4}, and \texttt{Id4} constants exported by
\emph{Echoes} are the \(6\times6\) Kelvin-Mandel representations of the
volumetric projector \(\mathbb{J}\), deviatoric projector
\(\mathbb{K}\), and symmetric identity \(\mathbb{I}\), respectively.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ SphereInISO(user\_inclusion):}
    \CommentTok{"""Spherical inhomogeneity in an isotropic matrix.}
\CommentTok{    Concentration tensors computed analytically via the Hill}
\CommentTok{    polarization tensor formula (Eq. eq{-}P{-}sphere{-}iso)."""}

    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, prop, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[]):}
        \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(fraction, symmetrize)}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ key, val }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ prop.items():}
\NormalTok{            user\_inclusion.set\_prop(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, key, val)}

    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ build\_all(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
        \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} reference medium {-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{        C0      }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ref.array}
\NormalTok{        S0      }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linalg.inv(C0)}
\NormalTok{        k0, mu0 }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ref.kmu}
        \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} inclusion stiffness {-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{        Ci }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.prop(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.refname).array}
        \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} Hill tensor for sphere in isotropic matrix (@eq{-}P{-}sphere{-}iso) {-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{        P  }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0 }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (J4 }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{3.}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0 }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ K4)}
        \CommentTok{\# {-}{-}{-} concentration tensors {-}{-}{-}}
\NormalTok{        eE }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linalg.inv(Id4 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ P.dot(Ci }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ C0))}
\NormalTok{        eS }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eE.dot(S0)}
\NormalTok{        sE }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Ci.dot(eE)}
\NormalTok{        sS }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Ci.dot(eS)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ \{}\StringTok{"eE"}\NormalTok{: eE, }\StringTok{"eS"}\NormalTok{: eS, }\StringTok{"sE"}\NormalTok{: sE, }\StringTok{"sS"}\NormalTok{: sS\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Verification against
\texttt{ellipsoid}}{Verification against ellipsoid}}\label{verification-against-ellipsoid}

For a spherical inclusion, \texttt{SphereInISO} must reproduce the same
concentration tensors as \texttt{ellipsoid(shape=spherical)}, whose Hill
tensor is computed automatically by \emph{Echoes}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ci }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_Enu(}\FloatTok{5.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{)    }\CommentTok{\# inclusion: E=5, nu=0.25}
\NormalTok{C0 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{3.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{2.}\NormalTok{)      }\CommentTok{\# matrix: k=3, mu=2}

\CommentTok{\# Built{-}in ellipsoid (Echoes computes P internally)}
\NormalTok{ell }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Ci\})}
\NormalTok{ell.set\_ref(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, C0)}

\CommentTok{\# User{-}defined sphere (P computed by hand in build\_all)}
\NormalTok{usr }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ SphereInISO(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Ci\})}
\NormalTok{usr.set\_ref(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, C0)}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ name, ref, usr\_val }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ [}
\NormalTok{        (}\StringTok{"eE"}\NormalTok{, ell.eE, usr.eE),}
\NormalTok{        (}\StringTok{"eS"}\NormalTok{, ell.eS, usr.eS),}
\NormalTok{        (}\StringTok{"sE"}\NormalTok{, ell.sE, usr.sE),}
\NormalTok{        (}\StringTok{"sS"}\NormalTok{, ell.sS, usr.sS)]:}
\NormalTok{    err }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(np.}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(ref }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ usr\_val))}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Max |ell.}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{name}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{ {-} usr.}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{name}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{| = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{err}\SpecialCharTok{:.2e\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Max |ell.eE - usr.eE| = 1.11e-16
Max |ell.eS - usr.eS| = 2.78e-17
Max |ell.sE - usr.sE| = 8.88e-16
Max |ell.sS - usr.sS| = 2.22e-16
\end{verbatim}

\(\,\)

\part{𝗥𝗩𝗘 𝗮𝗻𝗱 𝗵𝗼𝗺𝗼𝗴𝗲𝗻𝗶𝘇𝗮𝘁𝗶𝗼𝗻 𝘀𝗰𝗵𝗲𝗺𝗲𝘀}

\chapter{Representative Volume Element}\label{sec-rve}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Objectives}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

This tutorial shows how to build a Representative Volume Element (RVE)
in \emph{Echoes} by assembling phases (ellipsoidal inclusions, n-layer
spheres) with their volume fractions and properties.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Imports}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ echoes }\ImportTok{import} \OperatorTok{*}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ math}

\NormalTok{np.set\_printoptions(precision}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, suppress}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Construction of a
\texttt{rve}}{Construction of a rve}}\label{sec-rve-construction}

In \emph{Echoes} a \texttt{rve} object inherits from the Python
\texttt{dict} (dictionary) with string keys and phase values. A
\emph{phase} is either an \texttt{ellipsoid} (see
Section~\ref{sec-ellipsoid}), a \texttt{sphere\_nlayers} (see
Section~\ref{sec-sphere_nlayers}), or a \texttt{crack} (see
Chapter~\ref{sec-crack-object}).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.3\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{4_schemes/../img/ver_ell_spn.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-rve}Representative Volume Element}

\end{figure}%

A \texttt{rve} is defined with optional keyword-arguments:

\begin{verbatim}
myrve = rve(matrix=phasename, prop={"C": C, ...})
\end{verbatim}

where \texttt{phasename} is a string referring to the name of one of the
phases and the \texttt{prop} argument is a dictionary of tensors indexed
by property names. The \texttt{matrix} keyword designates:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  the phase playing the role of the \textbf{matrix} in matrix/inclusion
  schemes (e.g.~Mori-Tanaka),
\item
  the phase used to \textbf{initialize} the fixed-point algorithm in the
  self-consistent scheme (unless \texttt{prop} is provided, in which
  case that property is used as initial value).
\end{itemize}

\section{Defining phases}\label{defining-phases}

The \texttt{rve} is completed by defining phases:

\begin{verbatim}
myrve["PHASE1"] = ellipsoid(shape=..., prop=..., fraction=f1, symmetrize=[ISO])
myrve["PHASE2"] = ellipsoid(shape=..., prop=..., fraction=f2)
myrve["PHASE3"] = sphere_nlayers(..., fraction=f3)
myrve["CRACK"]  = crack(shape=spheroidal(ω), density=ε, prop={"C": tZ4})
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Notes}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  If the \texttt{matrix} keyword was not used in the definition of the
  \texttt{rve}, the first phase plays the role of the matrix by default
  if necessary.
\item
  The \texttt{fraction} keyword denotes the volume fraction within the
  \texttt{rve}. The sum of all fractions should be equal to 1. If it is
  not the case, the volume fraction of the matrix is adjusted.
\item
  The \texttt{symmetrize={[}ISO{]}} argument ``isotropifies'' the
  directions of the ellipsoid: although the ellipsoid is defined with a
  specified direction, the phase is considered to represent an isotropic
  distribution of orientations. This argument should be used only if the
  reference medium of the homogenization scheme is itself isotropic.
\item
  For \texttt{crack} phases the \texttt{fraction} keyword is \textbf{not
  used}. Instead, the \textbf{Budiansky--O'Connell crack density}
  \(\varepsilon = n\,a^3\) (see Chapter~\ref{sec-crack-object}) is set
  via the \texttt{density} keyword. The volume fraction of a crack
  family vanishes as the aspect ratio \(\omega \to 0\), so density is
  the relevant parameter.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Modifying phases}\label{modifying-phases}

Each phase can be modified after construction:

\begin{verbatim}
myrve["PHASE1"].fraction = ...
myrve["PHASE1"].shape = ...
myrve["PHASE1"].set_prop("C", C)
myrve["PHASE1"].symmetrize(ISO)
myrve["PHASE1"].set_param_eshelby(algo=NUMINT, epsroots=1.e-4,
                                   epsabs=1.e-4, epsrel=1.e-4, maxnb=100000)
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-tip-color}{\faLightbulb}\hspace{0.5em}{Tip}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\texttt{set\_param\_eshelby} can also be applied at the level of the
\texttt{rve}: \texttt{myrve.set\_param\_eshelby(...)}. In this case the
parameters apply to all ellipsoidal phases.

\end{tcolorbox}

\(\,\)

\chapter{Homogenization schemes}\label{sec-schemes}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Objectives}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

This tutorial presents the homogenization schemes implemented in
\emph{Echoes} to estimate the effective properties of composite
materials described by a RVE (see Chapter~\ref{sec-rve}). The key
difference between schemes lies in the choice of \textbf{reference
medium} and \textbf{remote field} used in the auxiliary Eshelby
problems.

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Imports}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ echoes }\ImportTok{import} \OperatorTok{*}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ math}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ matplotlib.pyplot }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ plt}

\NormalTok{np.set\_printoptions(precision}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, suppress}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Principle of homogenization
schemes}\label{sec-principle-schemes}

A RVE \(\Omega\) is composed of \(N\) phases
(\(\Omega = \cup_{r=1}^N \Omega_r\)), each phase \(r\) having stiffness
\(\uuuu{C}_r\) and volume fraction \(f_r\). Under homogeneous strain
boundary conditions (\(\uv{u} = \E \cdot \x\) on \(\partial\Omega\)), by
linearity the macroscopic stiffness satisfies:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-Chom-general}{
\uuuu{C}^\textrm{hom} = \sum_{r=1}^N f_r \uuuu{C}_r : \uuuu{A}_r
}\end{equation}

where \(\uuuu{A}_r\) is the \textbf{strain concentration tensor} of
phase \(r\), satisfying \(\sum_{r=1}^N f_r \uuuu{A}_r = \uuuu{I}\).

Each homogenization scheme approximates \(\uuuu{A}_r\) by solving an
\textbf{auxiliary Eshelby problem}: each phase is embedded individually
in an infinite reference medium \(\uuuu{C}^\textrm{ref}\) subjected to a
remote strain \(\E^0\).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\begin{figure}
\centering
\includegraphics[width=0.8\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio,alt={Decomposition into Eshelby problems}]{4_schemes/../img/ver_decomp_eshelby.png}
\caption{Decomposition into Eshelby problems}
\end{figure}

}

\caption{\label{fig-schemes-decomp}Principle: the RVE is approximated by
a superposition of single-inclusion Eshelby problems, each embedding one
phase type in the reference medium.}

\end{figure}%

The dilute concentration tensor of phase \(r\) in reference medium
\(\uuuu{C}^\textrm{ref}\) is:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-Adil}{
\uuuu{A}_r^{dil} = \left(\uuuu{I} + \uuuu{P}(\uuuu{C}^\textrm{ref}, \uu{A}_r) : \delta\uuuu{C}_r\right)^{-1}
\qquad \delta\uuuu{C}_r = \uuuu{C}_r - \uuuu{C}^\textrm{ref}
}\end{equation}

where \(\uuuu{P}(\uuuu{C}^\textrm{ref}, \uu{A}_r)\) is the Hill
polarization tensor depending on the reference medium and the inclusion
shape \(\uu{A}_r\) (see Chapter~\ref{sec-eshelby_hill}).

The \textbf{schemes differ} in the choice of \(\uuuu{C}^\textrm{ref}\)
and how the concentration tensors are assembled. The available schemes
in \emph{Echoes} are listed in the following table.

{\def\LTcaptype{none} % do not increment counter
\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2000}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1778}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.3778}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 6\tabcolsep) * \real{0.2444}}@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Keyword
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Scheme
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Reference medium
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Iterative
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
\texttt{VOIGT} & Voigt bound & --- & No \\
\texttt{REUSS} & Reuss bound & --- & No \\
\texttt{DIL} & Dilute (strain) & Matrix \(\uuuu{C}_0\) & No \\
\texttt{DILD} & Dilute (stress) & Matrix \(\uuuu{C}_0\) & No \\
\texttt{MT} & Mori-Tanaka & Matrix \(\uuuu{C}_0\) & No \\
\texttt{SC} & Self-consistent & Effective \(\uuuu{C}^\textrm{hom}\) &
Yes \\
\texttt{ASC} & Asymmetric self-consistent & Effective
\(\uuuu{C}^\textrm{hom}\) (inclusions) & Yes \\
\texttt{MAX} & Maxwell & Matrix \(\uuuu{C}_0\) & No \\
\texttt{PCW} & Ponte Castañeda-Willis & Matrix \(\uuuu{C}_0\) & No \\
\texttt{DIFF} & Differential & Evolving \(\uuuu{C}^\textrm{hom}(f)\) &
No (ODE) \\
\end{longtable}
}

\section{\texorpdfstring{The \texttt{homogenize}
function}{The homogenize function}}\label{sec-homogenize-function}

All schemes share the same Python interface:

\begin{verbatim}
Chom = homogenize(prop="C", rve=myrve, scheme=sch,
                  verbose=False, maxnb=100, epsrel=1.e-6)
\end{verbatim}

where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{prop} is the property name (\texttt{"C"} for stiffness,
  \texttt{"K"} for conductivity, \ldots)
\item
  \texttt{rve} is the RVE (see Chapter~\ref{sec-rve})
\item
  \texttt{scheme} is one of \texttt{VOIGT}, \texttt{REUSS},
  \texttt{DIL}, \texttt{DILD}, \texttt{MT}, \texttt{SC}, \texttt{ASC},
  \texttt{MAX}, \texttt{PCW}, \texttt{DIFF}
\item
  \texttt{verbose} prints iteration details (useful for iterative
  schemes)
\item
  \texttt{maxnb} and \texttt{epsrel} are the maximum number of
  iterations and relative tolerance for iterative schemes
\end{itemize}

The result is a \texttt{tensor\_param} object; for isotropic results,
\texttt{.k}, \texttt{.mu}, \texttt{.E}, \texttt{.nu} give the elastic
moduli.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The \texttt{concentration}
keyword}{The concentration keyword}}\label{sec-concentration-keyword}

Passing \texttt{concentration=True} stores the \textbf{strain
concentration tensors} on each inclusion after the call. More precisely,
each inclusion's \texttt{.eE} attribute holds \(\uuuu{A}_r^{dil}\) ---
the dilute (un-normalized) tensor. The actual concentration tensor
satisfying \(\sum_r f_r \uuuu{A}_r = \uuuu{I}\) is:

\[
\uuuu{A}_r = \uuuu{A}_r^{dil} : \left(\sum_s f_s \uuuu{A}_s^{dil}\right)^{-1}
\]

\textbf{Application --- Biot coefficient of a porous medium.} In linear
poromechanics the second-order \textbf{Biot tensor} \(\uu{b}\) links the
macroscopic strain to the pore-pressure loading
(\citeproc{ref-dormieux2006}{Dormieux et al., 2006}):

\[
\uu{b} = f_p\,\uu{I} : \uuuu{A}_p
\]

where \(f_p\) is the porosity and \(\uuuu{A}_p\) is the pore
strain-concentration tensor. In Kelvin-Mandel notation this is
\texttt{ver{[}"PORE"{]}.factor\ *\ Id2.dot(Ap)}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{f\_por }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.1}
\NormalTok{ver\_sp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ver\_sp[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{f\_por,}
\NormalTok{                             prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{32.}\NormalTok{)\})}
\NormalTok{ver\_sp[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{]  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{f\_por,}
\NormalTok{                             prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: tZ4\})}

\CommentTok{\# homogenize with concentration=True fills .eE on each inclusion}
\NormalTok{Chom\_sp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ver\_sp, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT,}
\NormalTok{                     concentration}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# normalize: A\_r = eE\_r · (Σ\_s f\_s eE\_s)\^{}\{{-}1\}}
\NormalTok{sum\_feE }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (ver\_sp[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].factor }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ ver\_sp[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].eE}
         \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ ver\_sp[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].factor  }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ ver\_sp[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].eE)}
\NormalTok{Ap }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ver\_sp[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].eE.dot(np.linalg.inv(sum\_feE))}

\CommentTok{\# Biot tensor b = f\_p * I : A\_p  (second{-}order, KM 6{-}vector)}
\CommentTok{\# For an isotropic result the first three components equal b\_scalar}
\NormalTok{b\_vec    }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ver\_sp[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].factor }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ Id2.dot(Ap)}
\NormalTok{b\_scalar }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.mean(b\_vec[:}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{])   }\CommentTok{\# = tr(b)/3 for isotropic b}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"k\_hom    = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{Chom\_sp}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.4f\}}\SpecialStringTok{ GPa"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Biot b (KM 6{-}vector): }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{b\_vec}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Biot b scalar (tensorial route) = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{b\_scalar}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\CommentTok{\# Cross{-}check: scalar formula b = 1 {-} k\_hom/k\_solid}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Scalar check  b = 1 {-} k\_hom/k\_s = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ Chom\_sp}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\OperatorTok{/}\FloatTok{72.}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
k_hom    = 55.4439 GPa
Biot b (KM 6-vector): [0.229947 0.229947 0.229947 0.       0.       0.      ]
Biot b scalar (tensorial route) = 0.229947
Scalar check  b = 1 - k_hom/k_s = 0.229947
\end{verbatim}

For an \textbf{isotropic} medium with a \textbf{homogeneous solid} (bulk
modulus \(k_s\)), the Biot coefficient simplifies to the scalar
\(b = 1 - k^{hom}/k_s\), which the tensorial route confirms. The general
formula via \texttt{concentration=True} is needed when the solid is
anisotropic, multi-phase, or when the pore shape is non-spherical
(anisotropic Biot tensor).

\section{\texorpdfstring{Voigt and Reuss bounds (\texttt{VOIGT},
\texttt{REUSS})}{Voigt and Reuss bounds (VOIGT, REUSS)}}\label{sec-voigt-reuss}

These are the simplest estimates and provide \textbf{strict bounds} on
the effective stiffness:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-voigt}{
\uuuu{C}^\textrm{Voigt} = \sum_{r=1}^N f_r \uuuu{C}_r
\qquad\text{(arithmetic mean — upper bound)}
}\end{equation}

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-reuss}{
\uuuu{C}^\textrm{Reuss} = \left(\sum_{r=1}^N f_r \uuuu{C}_r^{-1}\right)^{-1}
\qquad\text{(harmonic mean — lower bound)}
}\end{equation}

They correspond to the assumptions of uniform strain (Voigt) and uniform
stress (Reuss) throughout the RVE. These estimates are not accurate for
most real composites but serve as bounding references.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Two{-}phase porous medium: solid + pores}
\NormalTok{Cs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{32.}\NormalTok{)   }\CommentTok{\# solid phase}
\NormalTok{Cp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{1e{-}6}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1e{-}6}\NormalTok{) }\CommentTok{\# pore phase (nearly zero stiffness)}

\NormalTok{myrve }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                            prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cs\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{]  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                            prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cp\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{CV }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{VOIGT)}
\NormalTok{CR }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{REUSS)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Voigt: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CV}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CV}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Reuss: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CR}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CR}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Voigt: k=57.600, mu=25.600
Reuss: k=0.000, mu=0.000
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Dilute strain scheme
(\texttt{DIL})}{Dilute strain scheme (DIL)}}\label{sec-DIL}

The dilute scheme embeds each inclusion in the \textbf{matrix}
\(\uuuu{C}_0\), ignoring interactions between inclusions. The remote
strain equals the macroscopic strain (\(\E^0 = \E\)), which is valid
only at low inclusion concentrations. The effective stiffness is:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-DIL}{
\uuuu{C}^\textrm{DIL} = \uuuu{C}_0 + \sum_{r \neq 0} f_r \,\delta\uuuu{C}_r : \uuuu{A}_r^{dil}(\uuuu{C}_0)
}\end{equation}

where the sum runs over non-matrix phases only. Since mutual
interactions are neglected, the result can overestimate stiffness at
moderate to high concentrations.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{CDIL }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{DIL)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Dilute: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CDIL}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CDIL}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Dilute: k=33.300, mu=19.823
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Dilute stress scheme
(\texttt{DILD})}{Dilute stress scheme (DILD)}}\label{sec-DILD}

The dilute stress scheme is the \textbf{dual counterpart} of
\texttt{DIL}, formulated in terms of compliance. It applies a remote
stress \(\Sig^0 = \Sig\) (rather than a remote strain), which is more
natural when inclusions are much softer than the matrix. Working on
compliances \(\uuuu{S}_r = \uuuu{C}_r^{-1}\):

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-DILD}{
\uuuu{S}^\textrm{DILD} = \uuuu{S}_0 + \sum_{r \neq 0} f_r \,\delta\uuuu{S}_r : \uuuu{B}_r^{dil}(\uuuu{C}_0)
}\end{equation}

where \(\delta\uuuu{S}_r = \uuuu{S}_r - \uuuu{S}_0\) is the compliance
contrast and
\(\uuuu{B}_r^{dil} = (\uuuu{I} + \uuuu{Q}_0 : \delta\uuuu{S}_r)^{-1}\)
uses the dual Hill tensor
\(\uuuu{Q}_0 = \uuuu{C}_0 - \uuuu{C}_0 : \uuuu{P}_0 : \uuuu{C}_0\).

For the same porous medium, the two dilute schemes give different
results: \texttt{DILD} is stiffer than \texttt{DIL}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{CDILD }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{DILD)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Dilute (stress): k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CDILD}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CDILD}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Dilute (stress): k=46.829, mu=23.179
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Mori-Tanaka scheme
(\texttt{MT})}{Mori-Tanaka scheme (MT)}}\label{sec-MT}

The Mori-Tanaka scheme (\citeproc{ref-bornert2001a}{Bornert et al.,
2001}) is the most widely used matrix/inclusion scheme. It corrects the
dilute scheme by requiring that the strain concentration tensors sum to
the identity (\ref{eq-A-constraint}). The key idea: each inclusion sees
the \textbf{mean strain of the matrix phase} as its remote field, which
accounts for interactions in an average sense.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.35\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{4_schemes/../img/ver_ell_spn.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-MT}Mori-Tanaka: inclusions embedded in the matrix
(reference medium = matrix).}

\end{figure}%

The strain concentration tensors satisfy:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-A-mt}{
\uuuu{A}_r^{MT} = \uuuu{A}_r^{dil} : \left(\sum_{s=0}^{N} f_s \,\uuuu{A}_s^{dil}\right)^{-1}
\qquad \uuuu{A}_0^{dil} = \uuuu{I}
}\end{equation}

and the effective stiffness is:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-C-mt}{
\uuuu{C}^{MT} = \left(\sum_{r=0}^{N} f_r \,\uuuu{C}_r : \uuuu{A}_r^{dil}\right) : \left(\sum_{r=0}^{N} f_r \,\uuuu{A}_r^{dil}\right)^{-1}
}\end{equation}

The MT scheme is exact in the dilute limit and well-suited for
\textbf{matrix-inclusion} microstructures where the matrix is connected.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{CMT }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Mori{-}Tanaka: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CMT}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CMT}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Mori-Tanaka: k=43.065, mu=21.685
\end{verbatim}

The MT scheme also handles \textbf{multi-phase RVEs} with different
inclusion shapes, orientations and interface conditions:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Multi{-}phase example: spheres + spheroids + n{-}layer sphere}
\NormalTok{myrve3 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"MATRIX"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve3[}\StringTok{"MATRIX"}\NormalTok{]   }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical,}
\NormalTok{    prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{5.4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{3.2}\NormalTok{)\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve3[}\StringTok{"SPHEROIDS"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{    symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO], prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{1.4}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.6}\NormalTok{)\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve3[}\StringTok{"SPN"}\NormalTok{]      }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sphere\_nlayers(radius}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    layer\_fractions}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{], fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{    prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: [stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.3}\NormalTok{), stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{4.2}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{3.1}\NormalTok{)]\},}
\NormalTok{    interf\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: [[NODISC], [}\FloatTok{2.1}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.5}\NormalTok{, PRIMALDISC]]\})}

\NormalTok{CMT3 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve3, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(CMT3)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(CMT3.k,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(CMT3.mu,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{, "}
      \SpecialStringTok{f"E=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(CMT3.E,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{, nu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{round}\NormalTok{(CMT3.nu,}\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{)}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Order 4 ISO tensor | Param(size=2)=[ 9.75351 4.10819 ] | Angles(size=0)=[ ]
[ 5.98996 1.88177 1.88177 0 0 0 
  1.88177 5.98996 1.88177 0 0 0 
  1.88177 1.88177 5.98996 0 0 0 
  0 0 0 4.10819 0 0 
  0 0 0 0 4.10819 0 
  0 0 0 0 0 4.10819 ]

k=3.251, mu=2.054, E=5.09, nu=0.239
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Self-consistent scheme
(\texttt{SC})}{Self-consistent scheme (SC)}}\label{sec-SC}

The self-consistent scheme (\citeproc{ref-zaoui2002}{Zaoui, 2002}) is
adapted to \textbf{fully disordered} materials where no phase plays a
privileged role of connected matrix (e.g., polycrystals, granular
media). Each phase is embedded in the \textbf{homogenized medium
itself}:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-A-sc}{
\uuuu{A}_r^{SC} = \left(\uuuu{I} + \uuuu{P}(\uuuu{C}^{hom}) : \delta\uuuu{C}_r^{hom}\right)^{-1}
\qquad \delta\uuuu{C}_r^{hom} = \uuuu{C}_r - \uuuu{C}^{hom}
}\end{equation}

Since \(\uuuu{C}^{hom}\) appears on both sides, this gives the
\textbf{implicit} fixed-point equation:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-C-sc}{
\uuuu{C}^{SC} = \left(\sum_{r=1}^{N} f_r \,\uuuu{C}_r : \uuuu{A}_r^{SC}\right) : \left(\sum_{r=1}^{N} f_r \,\uuuu{A}_r^{SC}\right)^{-1}
}\end{equation}

solved iteratively. The SC scheme naturally predicts \textbf{percolation
thresholds} and handles topological transitions (e.g., the solid matrix
losing connectivity). The \texttt{maxnb} and \texttt{epsrel} parameters
control convergence.

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=0.3\linewidth,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{4_schemes/../img/verSC.png}

}

\caption{\label{fig-SC}Self-consistent: each grain is embedded in the
effective medium (no privileged matrix phase).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{CSC }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{SC,}
\NormalTok{                 verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}10}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Self{-}consistent: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CSC}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CSC}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Self-consistent: k=37.156, mu=19.629
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

The SC scheme predicts \textbf{zero stiffness} (percolation threshold)
when the pore fraction reaches a critical value (around 50\% for
spherical pores), unlike the MT scheme which only reaches zero at 100\%
porosity.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Asymmetric self-consistent scheme
(\texttt{ASC})}{Asymmetric self-consistent scheme (ASC)}}\label{sec-ASC}

The asymmetric self-consistent scheme (\citeproc{ref-saevik2014}{Saevik
et al., 2014}) is a variant of the SC scheme designed for
\textbf{matrix-inclusion} microstructures. Unlike SC (where all phases
--- including the matrix --- are embedded in the effective medium), ASC
treats the phases asymmetrically:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Inclusions} are embedded in the current effective medium
  \(\uuuu{C}^{hom}\) to compute their contribution
\item
  \textbf{The matrix} contributes directly as \(\uuuu{C}_0\)
\end{itemize}

This leads to the fixed-point equation:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-C-asc}{
\uuuu{C}^{ASC} = \uuuu{C}_0 + \sum_{r \neq 0} f_r \left(\uuuu{C}_r - \uuuu{C}^{ASC}\right) : \left(\uuuu{I} + \uuuu{P}(\uuuu{C}^{ASC}) : \left(\uuuu{C}_r - \uuuu{C}^{ASC}\right)\right)^{-1}
}\end{equation}

solved iteratively. The ASC scheme interpolates between MT (at low
concentrations) and SC behavior, while preserving the matrix/inclusion
topology at all concentrations. When inclusions are softer than the
matrix, the iteration is performed on compliances instead.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{CASC }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ASC,}
\NormalTok{                  verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}10}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Asymmetric SC: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CASC}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CASC}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Asymmetric SC: k=37.156, mu=19.629
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Maxwell scheme
(\texttt{MAX})}{Maxwell scheme (MAX)}}\label{sec-MAX}

The Maxwell scheme (\citeproc{ref-withers1989}{Withers, 1989}) considers
all inclusions enclosed in a single \textbf{outer ellipsoidal domain}
\(\Omega^{MAX}\) (the Maxwell domain) embedded in the matrix. It equates
the far-field disturbance produced by this domain (treated as an
equivalent single inhomogeneity) with the sum of individual disturbances
of each inclusion:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-C-max}{
\uuuu{C}^{MAX} = \uuuu{C}_0 + \left[\left(\sum_{r \neq 0} f_r \,\delta\uuuu{C}_r : \uuuu{A}_r^{dil}(\uuuu{C}_0)\right)^{-1} - \uuuu{P}(\uuuu{C}_0, \Omega^{MAX})\right]^{-1}
}\end{equation}

where \(\uuuu{P}(\uuuu{C}_0, \Omega^{MAX})\) is the Hill tensor of the
\textbf{Maxwell domain} (the outer ellipsoid defined by the RVE shape).
This scheme is explicit (non-iterative) and naturally satisfies the
exact dilute limit.

In \emph{Echoes}, the Maxwell domain shape is set by the \textbf{RVE's
own shape} (its \texttt{self()} ellipsoid). By default this is a sphere.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{CMAX }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MAX)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Maxwell: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CMAX}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CMAX}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Maxwell: k=43.065, mu=21.685
\end{verbatim}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

For \textbf{spherical inclusions in an isotropic matrix} with a
spherical Maxwell domain, the Maxwell and MT schemes give identical
results. Differences appear when the inclusion shapes or the Maxwell
domain are non-spherical.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Ponte Castañeda-Willis scheme
(\texttt{PCW})}{Ponte Castañeda-Willis scheme (PCW)}}\label{sec-PCW}

The Ponte Castañeda-Willis scheme (\citeproc{ref-willis1977}{Willis,
1977}) introduces a \textbf{distribution ellipsoid} \(\Omega^d\)
distinct from the inclusion shapes to model the \textbf{spatial
arrangement} of inclusions. This distribution captures whether
inclusions are clustered, layered, or randomly distributed.

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-C-pcw}{
\uuuu{C}^{PCW} = \uuuu{C}_0 + \left[\left(\sum_{r \neq 0} f_r \,\delta\uuuu{C}_r : \uuuu{A}_r^{dil}(\uuuu{C}_0)\right)^{-1} - \uuuu{P}(\uuuu{C}_0, \Omega^d)\right]^{-1}
}\end{equation}

The PCW formula is mathematically identical to the Maxwell formula
(\ref{eq-C-max}) with \(\Omega^d\) playing the role of \(\Omega^{MAX}\).
The distinction is conceptual:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Maxwell}: \(\Omega^{MAX}\) is the bounding ellipsoid of the
  inclusion cluster
\item
  \textbf{PCW}: \(\Omega^d\) describes the statistical distribution of
  inclusion centers
\end{itemize}

In particular, when \(\Omega^d\) coincides with the individual inclusion
shapes, the PCW scheme reduces to \textbf{Mori-Tanaka}. This confirms
that MT implicitly assumes inclusions are distributed with the same
ellipsoidal statistics as their shapes.

In \emph{Echoes}, the distribution ellipsoid is the \textbf{RVE's own
shape} (same as for MAX).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{CPCW }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{PCW)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"PCW: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CPCW}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CPCW}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
PCW: k=43.065, mu=21.685
\end{verbatim}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Differential scheme
(\texttt{DIFF})}{Differential scheme (DIFF)}}\label{sec-DIFF}

The differential scheme builds the composite \textbf{incrementally}:
starting from the pure matrix, a small fraction \(\ud f\) of inclusions
is added at each step and the effective properties are immediately
updated as the new reference. This leads to the ODE:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-DIFF-ode}{
\frac{\ud\uuuu{C}^{hom}}{\ud f} = \frac{1}{1-f} \left(\uuuu{C}_1 - \uuuu{C}^{hom}\right) : \left(\uuuu{I} + \uuuu{P}(\uuuu{C}^{hom}) : \left(\uuuu{C}_1 - \uuuu{C}^{hom}\right)\right)^{-1}
}\end{equation}

with \(\uuuu{C}^{hom}(0) = \uuuu{C}_0\). The \(1/(1-f)\) factor accounts
for the fact that each increment dilutes into the remaining matrix
volume. The ODE is integrated numerically up to the desired total
inclusion fraction.

The differential scheme is particularly useful at \textbf{high
concentrations} and avoids unphysical percolation artifacts. It predicts
zero stiffness for a porous medium only at 100\% porosity (like MT).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{CDIFF }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{DIFF)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"Differential: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CDIFF}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{k}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{, mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{CDIFF}\SpecialCharTok{.}\NormalTok{mu}\SpecialCharTok{:.3f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Differential: k=40.586, mu=20.859
\end{verbatim}

\section{Comparative example: porous
medium}\label{sec-schemes-comparison}

To illustrate the differences between all schemes, we plot the bulk
modulus \(k^{hom}\) and shear modulus \(\mu^{hom}\) of a
\textbf{spherical pore} porous medium as a function of porosity
\(\varphi\), for all available schemes.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ks, mus }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{32.}
\NormalTok{kp, mup }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}

\NormalTok{rve\_por }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{rve\_por[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                              prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(ks, mus)\})}
\NormalTok{rve\_por[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{]  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                              prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(kp, mup)\})}

\NormalTok{schemes }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [VOIGT, REUSS, DIL, DILD, MT, SC, ASC, MAX, DIFF]}
\NormalTok{labels  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\StringTok{"Voigt"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"Reuss"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"DIL"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"DILD"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"MT"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"SC"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"ASC"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"MAX=PCW"}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{"DIFF"}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{styles  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [}\StringTok{"r{-}{-}"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"b{-}{-}"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"g{-}"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"g:"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"k{-}"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"r{-}"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"r{-}."}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"b{-}"}\NormalTok{,}\StringTok{"y{-}"}\NormalTok{]}

\NormalTok{phi\_list }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linspace(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{101}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{fig, (ax1, ax2) }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ plt.subplots(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ sch, lbl, sty }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{zip}\NormalTok{(schemes, labels, styles):}
\NormalTok{    lk, lmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [], []}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ phi }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ phi\_list:}
\NormalTok{        rve\_por[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].fraction  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ phi}
\NormalTok{        rve\_por[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].fraction }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ phi}
        \ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{rve\_por, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch,}
\NormalTok{                           verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}8}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{            lk.append(}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(C.k, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{            lmu.append(}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(C.mu, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{))}
        \ControlFlowTok{except} \PreprocessorTok{Exception}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            lk.append(}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"nan"}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{            lmu.append(}\BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"nan"}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{    ax1.plot(phi\_list, lk,  sty, label}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{lbl)}
\NormalTok{    ax2.plot(phi\_list, lmu, sty, label}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{lbl)}

\NormalTok{ax1.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"Porosity }\DecValTok{$}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}v}\VerbatimStringTok{arphi}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax1.set\_ylabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{k}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ax1.set\_title(}\StringTok{"Bulk modulus"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax1.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax1.legend()}
\NormalTok{ax2.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"Porosity }\DecValTok{$}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}v}\VerbatimStringTok{arphi}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax2.set\_ylabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ax2.set\_title(}\StringTok{"Shear modulus"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax2.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax2.legend()}
\NormalTok{plt.tight\_layout()}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{4_schemes/schemes_files/figure-pdf/fig-comparison-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-comparison}Effective bulk and shear moduli
vs.~porosity for all schemes (solid: \(k=72\), \(\mu=32\); spherical
pores).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-note-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-note-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title=\textcolor{quarto-callout-note-color}{\faInfo}\hspace{0.5em}{Note}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

Key observations from the comparison:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Voigt/Reuss} are the extremal bounds; all other schemes lie
  between them.
\item
  \textbf{DIL/DILD} deviate from the bounds for large porosity and can
  give non-physical results (negative moduli).
\item
  \textbf{MT} is the natural choice for a solid matrix with embedded
  pores; it predicts zero stiffness only at \(\varphi=1\).
\item
  \textbf{SC} predicts a \textbf{percolation threshold} around
  \(\varphi \approx 0.5\) (zero stiffness for spherical pores).
\item
  \textbf{ASC} lies between MT and SC; it preserves the matrix topology
  while using the effective medium as reference for inclusions.
\item
  \textbf{MAX/PCW} coincide with MT for spherical inclusions in an
  isotropic matrix (when the distribution ellipsoid is spherical).
\item
  \textbf{DIFF} gives a smooth transition to zero, always positive,
  making it robust at high concentrations.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{\texorpdfstring{Using a \texttt{user\_inclusion} in a
scheme}{Using a user\_inclusion in a scheme}}\label{sec-user-in-scheme}

A \texttt{user\_inclusion} (see Chapter~\ref{sec-user_inclusion}) is
used \textbf{exactly like any other inclusion} inside an \texttt{rve}:
it is assigned a fraction, stored in the \texttt{rve} dictionary, and
passed to \texttt{homogenize}. The homogenization engine calls
\texttt{set\_ref} (which in turn triggers \texttt{build\_all})
automatically before each evaluation of the concentration tensors.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Cp\_u }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{)   }\CommentTok{\# near{-}zero stiffness for pores}
\NormalTok{Cs\_u }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{32.}\NormalTok{)        }\CommentTok{\# solid mineral}

\KeywordTok{class}\NormalTok{ SphereInISO(user\_inclusion):}
    \CommentTok{"""Spherical inhomogeneity in an isotropic matrix.}
\CommentTok{    Concentration tensors computed analytically via the Hill}
\CommentTok{    polarization tensor formula."""}
    \KeywordTok{def} \FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, prop, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[]):}
        \BuiltInTok{super}\NormalTok{().}\FunctionTok{\_\_init\_\_}\NormalTok{(fraction, symmetrize)}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ key, val }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ prop.items():}
\NormalTok{            user\_inclusion.set\_prop(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{, key, val)}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ build\_all(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{        C0      }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ref.array}
\NormalTok{        S0      }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linalg.inv(C0)}
\NormalTok{        k0, mu0 }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.ref.kmu}
\NormalTok{        Ci }\OperatorTok{=} \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.prop(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.refname).array}
\NormalTok{        P  }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0 }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (J4 }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{3.}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0 }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ K4)}
\NormalTok{        eE }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linalg.inv(Id4 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ P.dot(Ci }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ C0))}
\NormalTok{        eS }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ eE.dot(S0)}
\NormalTok{        sE }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Ci.dot(eE)}
\NormalTok{        sS }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Ci.dot(eS)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ \{}\StringTok{"eE"}\NormalTok{: eE, }\StringTok{"eS"}\NormalTok{: eS, }\StringTok{"sE"}\NormalTok{: sE, }\StringTok{"sS"}\NormalTok{: sS\}}

\CommentTok{\# RVE: two user\_inclusions (solid matrix + spherical pores)}
\NormalTok{ver\_usr }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ver\_usr[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ SphereInISO(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cs\_u\})}
\NormalTok{ver\_usr[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{]  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ SphereInISO(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cp\_u\})}

\CommentTok{\# Cross{-}check: same RVE with built{-}in ellipsoids}
\NormalTok{ver\_ell }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ver\_ell[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO], prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cs\_u\})}
\NormalTok{ver\_ell[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{]  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO], prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cp\_u\})}

\NormalTok{phi\_test }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.3}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ sch }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ [MT, SC]:}
\NormalTok{    ver\_usr[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].fraction }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ phi\_test}
\NormalTok{    ku, muu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ver\_usr, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{).kmu}
\NormalTok{    ver\_ell[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].fraction }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ phi\_test}
\NormalTok{    ke, mue }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ver\_ell, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{).kmu}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{(sch)}\SpecialCharTok{:4s\}}\SpecialStringTok{  user\_inclusion: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{ku}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{  mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{muu}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{  |  "}
          \SpecialStringTok{f"ellipsoid: k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{ke}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{  mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{mue}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{  |  "}
          \SpecialStringTok{f"err\_k=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(ku}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ke)}\SpecialCharTok{:.1e\}}\SpecialStringTok{  err\_mu=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(muu}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{mue)}\SpecialCharTok{:.1e\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
MT    user_inclusion: k=33.460582  mu=17.626742  |  ellipsoid: k=33.460582  mu=17.626742  |  err_k=0.0e+00  err_mu=0.0e+00
SC    user_inclusion: k=22.689245  mu=13.264364  |  ellipsoid: k=22.689245  mu=13.264364  |  err_k=0.0e+00  err_mu=5.3e-15
\end{verbatim}

\(\,\)

\chapter{Homogenization of porous media}\label{sec-porous-media}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Objectives}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

This tutorial demonstrates the application of homogenization schemes to
porous media. It covers the effect of pore shape, the comparison of all
available schemes, percolation thresholds, and the extension to
transport properties (permeability).

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Imports}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ echoes }\ImportTok{import} \OperatorTok{*}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ math}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ matplotlib.pyplot }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ plt}

\NormalTok{np.set\_printoptions(precision}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, suppress}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Porous medium modeling}\label{porous-medium-modeling}

A porous medium is modeled as a composite in which the voids are treated
as inclusions with zero (or near-zero) stiffness:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ks, mus }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{32.}
\NormalTok{kp, mup }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}

\NormalTok{myrve }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                           prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(ks, mus)\})}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                          prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(kp, mup)\})}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Scheme comparison on porous media}\label{sec-porous-comparison}

The following example computes the effective bulk and shear moduli
versus porosity for all available schemes.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ Chom\_porous(myrve, phi, sch):}
\NormalTok{    myrve[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].fraction }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ phi}
\NormalTok{    myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].fraction }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ phi}
    \ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{        C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch,}
\NormalTok{                       verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                       select\_best}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(C.k, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{), }\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(C.mu, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{)}
    \ControlFlowTok{except}\NormalTok{:}
        \ControlFlowTok{return} \FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.}

\NormalTok{lphi }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linspace(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{51}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{fig, (ax1, ax2) }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ plt.subplots(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ sch }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ [VOIGT, REUSS, DIL, MT, SC, DIFF, PCW, MAX]:}
\NormalTok{    lk, lmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [], []}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ phi }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ lphi:}
\NormalTok{        k, mu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Chom\_porous(myrve, phi, sch)}
\NormalTok{        lk.append(k)}
\NormalTok{        lmu.append(mu)}
\NormalTok{    ax1.plot(lphi, lk, label}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{(sch))}
\NormalTok{    ax2.plot(lphi, lmu, label}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{(sch))}

\NormalTok{ax1.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}v}\VerbatimStringTok{arphi}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax1.set\_ylabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{k}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ax1.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax1.legend()}
\NormalTok{ax2.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}v}\VerbatimStringTok{arphi}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax2.set\_ylabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ax2.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax2.legend()}
\NormalTok{plt.tight\_layout()}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{5_cracked_porous/porous_media_files/figure-pdf/cell-4-output-1.pdf}}

\section{Effect of pore shape}\label{effect-of-pore-shape}

The aspect ratio of pores significantly affects the effective
properties. Oblate pores (\(\omega < 1\)) lead to softer behavior than
spherical pores (\(\omega = 1\)), while prolate pores (\(\omega > 1\))
produce an intermediate effect.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{lphi }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linspace(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{51}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{fig, (ax1, ax2) }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ plt.subplots(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ omega, style }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ [(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}{-}{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{), (}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{), (}\FloatTok{10.}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}:\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)]:}
\NormalTok{    myrve[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].shape }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ spheroidal(omega)}
\NormalTok{    myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].shape }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ spheroidal(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    lk, lmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [], []}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ phi }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ lphi:}
\NormalTok{        k, mu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Chom\_porous(myrve, phi, SC)}
\NormalTok{        lk.append(k)}
\NormalTok{        lmu.append(mu)}
\NormalTok{    ax1.plot(lphi, lk, style, label}\OperatorTok{=}\SpecialStringTok{f"SC, ω=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{omega}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ax2.plot(lphi, lmu, style, label}\OperatorTok{=}\SpecialStringTok{f"SC, ω=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{omega}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{ax1.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}v}\VerbatimStringTok{arphi}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax1.set\_ylabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{k}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ax1.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax1.legend()}
\NormalTok{ax2.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}v}\VerbatimStringTok{arphi}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax2.set\_ylabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ax2.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax2.legend()}
\NormalTok{plt.tight\_layout()}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{5_cracked_porous/porous_media_files/figure-pdf/cell-5-output-1.pdf}}

\section{Percolation threshold}\label{percolation-threshold}

In the self-consistent scheme, the effective moduli vanish at a critical
porosity (percolation threshold) that depends on the pore aspect ratio.
For spherical pores (\(\omega=1\)), the percolation threshold is
\(\phi_c=0.5\). For oblate pores (\(\omega \to 0\)), the threshold tends
to 1, while for prolate pores (\(\omega \to \infty\)), it tends to 0.

\section{Permeability of porous media}\label{sec-permeability}

Transport properties (permeability, conductivity, diffusivity) are
described by 2nd-order tensors and can be homogenized with the same
framework using \texttt{prop="K"}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Ks }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.1} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tId2    }\CommentTok{\# solid conductivity}
\NormalTok{Kp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ tId2           }\CommentTok{\# pore conductivity}

\NormalTok{myrve\_K }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve\_K[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                             prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"K"}\NormalTok{: Ks\})}
\NormalTok{myrve\_K[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(}\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                            prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"K"}\NormalTok{: Kp\})}

\NormalTok{lphi }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linspace(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{51}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{fig, ax }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ plt.subplots(figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{))}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ sch }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ [MT, SC, MAX, PCW]:}
\NormalTok{    lK }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ phi }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ lphi:}
\NormalTok{        myrve\_K[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{].fraction }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ phi}
\NormalTok{        myrve\_K[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].fraction }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ phi}
        \ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            K }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"K"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve\_K, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch,}
\NormalTok{                           verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}10}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                           select\_best}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{            lK.append(}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(np.trace(K.array) }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{3.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{))}
        \ControlFlowTok{except}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            lK.append(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ax.plot(lphi, lK, label}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{(sch))}

\NormalTok{ax.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}v}\VerbatimStringTok{arphi}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax.set\_ylabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{K}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ax.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ax.legend()}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{5_cracked_porous/porous_media_files/figure-pdf/cell-6-output-1.pdf}}

\(\,\)

\chapter{Homogenization of cracked media}\label{sec-cracked-media}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Objectives}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

This tutorial presents the homogenization of cracked media in
\emph{Echoes} (the \texttt{crack} object is introduced in
Chapter~\ref{sec-crack-object}):

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  effective elastic properties of a randomly cracked isotropic medium
  with various schemes;
\item
  \textbf{transversely isotropic crack distribution} with
  \texttt{symmetrize={[}TI,\ θ,\ φ{]}};
\item
  the \textbf{linear spring interface model} and its effect on effective
  stiffness;
\item
  \textbf{coupled elastic and transport} properties for randomly cracked
  and multiple crack families.
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Imports}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ echoes }\ImportTok{import} \OperatorTok{*}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ math}

\NormalTok{np.set\_printoptions(precision}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, suppress}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Isotropic randomly cracked
medium}\label{isotropic-randomly-cracked-medium}

For randomly oriented penny-shaped cracks in an isotropic matrix, the
\texttt{symmetrize={[}ISO{]}} option projects the concentration tensor
onto the isotropic symmetry class. This yields scalar effective bulk and
shear moduli.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ matplotlib.pyplot }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ plt}

\NormalTok{ω }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e{-}3}
\NormalTok{Cs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_Enu(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{)   }\CommentTok{\# matrix: E=1, ν=0.2}

\NormalTok{myrve }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                            prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cs, }\StringTok{"K"}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tId2\})}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"CRACK"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ crack(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(ω), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                        prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: tZ4, }\StringTok{"K"}\NormalTok{: tensor(ω, ω, }\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{)\})}

\NormalTok{ld }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linspace(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{61}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{fig, axes }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ plt.subplots(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ sch, ls }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{zip}\NormalTok{([MT, SC, ASC, MAX, DIFF, PCW],}
\NormalTok{                   [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}{-}{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}{-}{-}\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}{-}.\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}:\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{]):}
\NormalTok{    lk, lmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ [], []}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ d }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ ld:}
\NormalTok{        myrve[}\StringTok{"CRACK"}\NormalTok{].density }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ d}
\NormalTok{        myrve.set\_prop(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, Cs)}
        \ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch,}
\NormalTok{                           verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{300}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                           select\_best}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{            lk.append(}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(C.k }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ Cs.k, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{            lmu.append(}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(C.mu }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ Cs.mu, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{))}
        \ControlFlowTok{except} \PreprocessorTok{Exception}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{            lk.append(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{            lmu.append(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    axes[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{].plot(ld, lk, ls, label}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{(sch))}
\NormalTok{    axes[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{].plot(ld, lmu, ls, label}\OperatorTok{=}\BuiltInTok{str}\NormalTok{(sch))}

\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ ax, lbl }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{zip}\NormalTok{(axes, [}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{k}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}/k}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{s}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{, }\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{\{hom\}/}\DecValTok{\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\DecValTok{\^{}}\VerbatimStringTok{s}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{]):}
\NormalTok{    ax.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r"}\DecValTok{$}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}v}\VerbatimStringTok{arepsilon}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ax.set\_ylabel(lbl)}
\NormalTok{    ax.set\_ylim(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.05}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ax.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ax.legend(loc}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"best"}\NormalTok{, fontsize}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{plt.tight\_layout()}
\NormalTok{plt.show()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{figure}[H]

\centering{

\pandocbounded{\includegraphics[keepaspectratio]{5_cracked_porous/cracked_media_files/figure-pdf/fig-cracked-iso-output-1.pdf}}

}

\caption{\label{fig-cracked-iso}Effective moduli of a randomly cracked
isotropic medium}

\end{figure}%

\section{Transversely isotropic crack distribution}\label{sec-ti-cracks}

When crack normals are not fully random but distributed with TI symmetry
around an axis, \texttt{symmetrize={[}TI,\ θ,\ φ{]}} is used instead of
\texttt{{[}ISO{]}}. A representative case is that of cracks whose
normals lie uniformly in a plane --- obtained by taking a single crack
orientation and averaging over rotations around the perpendicular axis.

The example below uses \texttt{ellipsoidal(1.,\ ω,\ 1./ω)} --- a crack
thin in \(\uv{e}_2\) (normal \(= \uv{e}_2\)) and elongated in
\(\uv{e}_3\). \texttt{symmetrize={[}TI,\ 0.,\ 0.{]}} then averages over
rotations around \(\uv{e}_3\), sweeping crack normals through the
\((\uv{e}_1,\uv{e}_2)\) plane. The resulting effective medium is TI with
axis \(\uv{e}_3\): the in-plane stiffness \(C_{1111}\) is strongly
degraded while the longitudinal conductivity \(K_{11}\) is enhanced by
the high normal conductance \(K_n \gg 1\) of the cracks.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ω }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e{-}4} \OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ ko }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e{-}10} \OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ Kt }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{ Kn }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e10}
\NormalTok{Cs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_Enu(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{C11s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Cs.array[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]}

\NormalTok{myrve\_ti }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve\_ti[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{                               prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cs, }\StringTok{"K"}\NormalTok{: ko }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ tId2\})}
\CommentTok{\# Crack: normal along e2 (thin dimension), elongated in e3}
\CommentTok{\# TI averaging around e3 → normals sweep (e1,e2) plane}
\NormalTok{myrve\_ti[}\StringTok{"CRACK"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ crack(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ellipsoidal(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{, ω, }\FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ω, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{                           symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[TI, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{],}
\NormalTok{                           prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: tZ4, }\StringTok{"K"}\NormalTok{: tensor(Kt, Kn, Kt)\})}

\NormalTok{ld }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.linspace(}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{101}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{fig, axes }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ plt.subplots(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, figsize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}
