\chapter{Time-domain viscoelastic
homogenization}\label{sec-viscoelasticity-time}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Objectives}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

This chapter presents the time-domain approach to homogenization of
ageing linear viscoelastic composites, following
(\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJES2019}{Barthélémy et al., 2019};
\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJSS2016}{Barthélémy et al., 2016};
\citeproc{ref-sanahuja2013}{Julien Sanahuja, 2013}). It covers:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  The \textbf{Volterra operator formalism} for ageing linear
  viscoelasticity and why ageing materials require a direct time-domain
  treatment,
\item
  The \textbf{numerical discretization} of Volterra integrals via
  trapezoidal block matrices (\citeproc{ref-sanahuja2013}{Julien
  Sanahuja, 2013}),
\item
  The \textbf{ALV Hill (polarization) tensor kernel}
  (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJSS2016}{Barthélémy et al., 2016}) and its
  block property (see also Appendix~\ref{sec-hill_alv}),
\item
  \textbf{Homogenization schemes} (Maxwell, DIL, MT, SC) for ellipsoidal
  inclusions in ALV media (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJES2019}{Barthélémy
  et al., 2019}),
\item
  The \emph{Echoes} commands \texttt{visco\_law} and
  \texttt{homogenize\_visco} for practical computations,
\item
  Validation against the Laplace-Carson approach
  (Chapter~\ref{sec-complex-moduli}) for a non-ageing PMMA composite,
\item
  Application to an ageing matrix--inclusion composite with a
  Kelvin-chain creep law (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJES2019}{Barthélémy
  et al., 2019}).
\end{itemize}

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Imports}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{import} \OperatorTok{*}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ matplotlib.pyplot }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ plt}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ echoes }\ImportTok{import} \OperatorTok{*}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ mpmath }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ mp}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ sys}
\NormalTok{sys.path.insert(}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}mittag\_leffler\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ mittag\_leffler }\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ ml }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ mittag\_leffler, I\_Rabotnov}

\NormalTok{mp.dps }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{25}
\NormalTok{set\_printoptions(precision}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, suppress}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The Mittag-Leffler function is provided by the
\href{https://github.com/khinsen/mittag-leffler}{\texttt{mittag-leffler}}
package (\citeproc{ref-hinsen2025}{Hinsen, 2025}), placed in the
\texttt{mittag\_leffler/} subfolder alongside this chapter.

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Volterra operator formalism}\label{sec-volterra-operator}

\subsection{Hereditary law and Volterra
operator}\label{hereditary-law-and-volterra-operator}

A \textbf{linear causal} material stores the full history of loading.
Under uniaxial stress, the strain at time \(t\) due to an infinitesimal
stress increment \(\ud\sigma(t')\) applied at past time \(t' \le t\)
equals \(J(t,t')\,\ud\sigma(t')\). Integrating over all past increments
(Stieltjes integral) gives the \textbf{hereditary law}:

\[
\varepsilon(t) = \int_{-\infty}^{t} J(t,t')\,\ud\sigma(t')
\]

The compact notation \(\varepsilon = J \circ \sigma\) defines the
\textbf{Volterra operator} associated with the kernel \(J(t,t')\). For
4th-order tensor kernels, the contraction over the tensor indices gives
the \textbf{tensor Volterra double contraction}:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-volterra-creep}{
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(t) = \uuuu{J}(t,\bullet) \dcirc \boldsymbol{\sigma}(\bullet)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
\varepsilon_{ij}(t) = \int_{-\infty}^{t} J_{ijkl}(t,t')\,\ud\sigma_{kl}(t')
}\end{equation}

The dual \textbf{relaxation} form writes
\(\boldsymbol{\sigma} = \uuuu{C} \dcirc \boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\),
i.e.:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-volterra-relax}{
\sigma_{ij}(t) = \int_{-\infty}^{t} C_{ijkl}(t,t')\,\ud\varepsilon_{kl}(t')
}\end{equation}

\subsection{Product of Volterra kernels and
inverse}\label{product-of-volterra-kernels-and-inverse}

The \textbf{product} of two scalar Volterra kernels \(F\) and \(G\) is
defined by:

\[(F \circ G)(t,t') = \int_{-\infty}^{t} F(t,\tau)\,\frac{\partial G}{\partial \tau}(\tau,t')\,\ud\tau\]

This product is associative and distributive over addition, but
\textbf{not commutative}. The \textbf{identity} for this product is the
Heaviside function \(H(t,t') = H(t-t')\) (step function). The
\textbf{Volterra inverse} \(\volt{F}\) of a kernel \(F\) satisfies:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-volterra-inverse}{
\volt{F} \circ F = F \circ \volt{F} = H
}\end{equation}

For 4th-order tensor kernels, the tensor Volterra inverse
\(\volt{\uuuu{C}}\) satisfies
\(\volt{\uuuu{C}} \dcirc \uuuu{C} = H\,\uuuu{I}\) (where \(\uuuu{I}\) is
the 4th-order identity tensor), so that \(\uuuu{J} = \volt{\uuuu{C}}\)
is the creep compliance and \(\uuuu{C} = \volt{\uuuu{J}}\) is the
relaxation tensor.

\subsection{Ageing vs.~non-ageing}\label{ageing-vs.-non-ageing}

For a \textbf{non-ageing} material, the kernel depends only on the time
difference: \(\uuuu{C}(t,t') = \uuuu{C}(t-t')\). The Volterra product
reduces to a convolution, and the \textbf{Laplace-Carson transform}
\(\tilde{\cal F}(p) = p\int_0^\infty e^{-pt}{\cal F}(t)\,\ud t\)
converts the hereditary law into an algebraic relation
\(\tilde{\boldsymbol{\sigma}}(p) = \tilde{\uuuu{C}}(p):\tilde{\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}}(p)\).
The correspondence principle then extends elastic homogenization results
directly (see Chapter~\ref{sec-complex-moduli}).

For an \textbf{ageing} material, \(\uuuu{C}(t,t')\) depends on both
\(t\) and \(t'\) independently. The convolution structure is broken, the
Laplace-Carson approach fails, and a direct time-domain treatment is
required. This is the focus of the present chapter.

\section{Numerical discretization}\label{sec-visco-discretization}

\subsection{Block matrix representation of Volterra
integrals}\label{block-matrix-representation-of-volterra-integrals}

Over a discrete time series \(t_0 < t_1 < \cdots < t_n\), the Volterra
integral is approximated by a finite sum. Define the \textbf{block
relaxation matrix} of phase \(r\) as the lower-triangular block matrix:

\[
\mathbf{R}^r = \bigl([\mathbf{R}^r]_{ij}\bigr)_{0\le j\le i\le n},
\qquad [\mathbf{R}^r]_{ij} = \uuuu{C}^r(t_i,t_j) \in \mathbb{R}^{6\times 6}
\]

The discrete constitutive law
\(\boldsymbol{\Sigma} = \mathbf{R}^r\,\boldsymbol{E}\) (block
matrix--vector product) is an approximation of the Volterra integral
\ref{eq-volterra-relax}, where \(\boldsymbol{\Sigma}\) and
\(\boldsymbol{E}\) are block vectors of stresses and strains at the
discrete times.

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Trapezoidal discretization
(\citeproc{ref-sanahuja2013}{Julien Sanahuja,
2013})}{Trapezoidal discretization (Julien Sanahuja, 2013)}}\label{trapezoidal-discretization-sanahuja2013}

Starting from \ref{eq-volterra-relax} with zero initial conditions
before \(t_0\), integrate by parts and apply the trapezoidal rule on
each interval \([t_j, t_{j+1}]\). The result is
\(\sigma(t_i) = \sum_{k=0}^{i} M_{ik}\,\varepsilon(t_k)\) with:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-visco-mat}{
M_{ik} = \begin{cases}
R(t_0,\,t_0) & i=k=0 \\[4pt]
\dfrac{1}{2}\bigl[R(t_i,t_{i-1}) + R(t_i,t_i)\bigr] & i=k>0 \\[4pt]
\dfrac{1}{2}\bigl[R(t_i,t_0) - R(t_i,t_1)\bigr] & i>0,\;k=0 \\[4pt]
\dfrac{1}{2}\bigl[R(t_i,t_{k-1}) - R(t_i,t_{k+1})\bigr] & i>k>0
\end{cases}
}\end{equation}

The lower-triangular structure reflects \textbf{causality}:
\(\sigma(t_i)\) depends only on \(\varepsilon(t_k)\) for \(k \le i\).

\subsection{Python implementation}\label{python-implementation}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ visco\_mat(f, t):}
    \CommentTok{"""Lower{-}triangular discretization matrix for scalar kernel f(t, t\textquotesingle{}).}
\CommentTok{    Implements Eq. (visco{-}mat) with zero initial conditions."""}
\NormalTok{    n }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{len}\NormalTok{(t)}
\NormalTok{    M }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ zeros((n, n))}
\NormalTok{    M[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ f(t[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{], t[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{])}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, n):}
\NormalTok{        M[i, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (f(t[i], t[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ f(t[i], t[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]))}
\NormalTok{        M[i, i] }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (f(t[i], t[i}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ f(t[i], t[i]))}
    \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, n):}
        \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ j }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, i):}
\NormalTok{            M[i, j] }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ (f(t[i], t[j}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]) }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ f(t[i], t[j}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{]))}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ M}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For illustration, consider a Maxwell relaxation
\(R(t,t') = e^{-(t-t')/\tau}\) with \(\tau=20\):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{tau\_m }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{20.}
\NormalTok{R\_maxwell }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{lambda}\NormalTok{ t, tp: exp(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(t }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ tp) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ tau\_m)}
\NormalTok{t\_small }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ array([}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{5.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{15.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{40.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{80.}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{M }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_mat(R\_maxwell, t\_small)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Relaxation matrix M (lower triangular):"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(M)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{Creep matrix M⁻¹:"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(linalg.inv(M))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Relaxation matrix M (lower triangular):
[[ 1.        0.        0.        0.        0.      ]
 [-0.1106    0.8894    0.        0.        0.      ]
 [-0.067082 -0.263817  0.803265  0.        0.      ]
 [-0.019219 -0.075585 -0.413113  0.643252  0.      ]
 [-0.002601 -0.010229 -0.055909 -0.480613  0.567668]]

Creep matrix M⁻¹:
[[1.       0.       0.       0.       0.      ]
 [0.124353 1.124353 0.       0.       0.      ]
 [0.124353 0.369272 1.244919 0.       0.      ]
 [0.124353 0.369272 0.799518 1.5546   0.      ]
 [0.124353 0.369272 0.799518 1.316194 1.761594]]
\end{verbatim}

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{The \texttt{visco\_law} object in
\emph{Echoes}}{The visco\_law object in Echoes}}\label{the-visco_law-object-in-echoes}

The 6×6 block version of \ref{eq-visco-mat} --- where each scalar entry
becomes a \(6\times 6\) tensor block --- is built by
\texttt{visco\_law(func,\ MODE)}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Cs }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ stiff\_Enu(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  ks, mus }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Cs.kmu}
\NormalTok{fk  }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{lambda}\NormalTok{ t: }\FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ exp(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{t }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{20.}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{0.5}   \CommentTok{\# ageing factor}
\NormalTok{fmu }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{lambda}\NormalTok{ t: }\FloatTok{0.5} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ exp(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{t }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{20.}\NormalTok{) }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{0.5}

\NormalTok{Js }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{lambda}\NormalTok{ t, tp: (fk(tp)  }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ks)  }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{0.1}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ks  }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ log(}\FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (t}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{tp) }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{2.}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ J4 }\OperatorTok{\textbackslash{}}
                 \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (fmu(tp) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mus) }\OperatorTok{+} \FloatTok{0.1}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{mus }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ log(}\FloatTok{1.} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (t}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{tp) }\OperatorTok{/} \FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{)) }\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ K4}

\NormalTok{vl }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_law(Js, CREEP)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The call \texttt{vl.mat(t)} returns the block creep matrix of size
\(6(n+1)\times 6(n+1)\); \texttt{vl.relaxation\_mat(t)} returns the
corresponding block relaxation matrix (Volterra inverse, computed
numerically). Let us display the \((1,1)\)-component of each block for a
4-step series:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{t3 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ array([}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{5.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{15.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{40.}\NormalTok{])}
\NormalTok{M\_block }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ vl.mat(t3)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Creep block matrix — (11){-}component of each 6×6 block:"}\NormalTok{)}
\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ i }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{    row }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{"  "}\NormalTok{.join(}\SpecialStringTok{f"}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{M\_block[}\DecValTok{6}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{i, }\DecValTok{6}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{j]}\SpecialCharTok{:8.5f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"} \ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ j }\KeywordTok{in} \BuiltInTok{range}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{))}
    \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"  t=}\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{t3[i]}\SpecialCharTok{:4.0f\}}\SpecialStringTok{:  }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{row}\SpecialCharTok{\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Creep block matrix — (11)-component of each 6×6 block:
  t=   0:   1.00000   0.00000   0.00000   0.00000
  t=   5:   0.23622   1.12562   0.00000   0.00000
  t=  15:   0.09572   0.41792   1.05838   0.00000
  t=  40:   0.06951   0.18160   0.53508   0.99065
\end{verbatim}

The matrix is strictly lower-triangular:
\(\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(t_i)\) depends only on past stresses. The
diagonal entries \(J(t_i,t_i)\) decrease with age \(t_i\) (the material
stiffens over time).

\subsection{\texorpdfstring{Symmetry decomposition:
\texttt{visco\_paramsym} and
\texttt{visco\_tensor}}{Symmetry decomposition: visco\_paramsym and visco\_tensor}}\label{sec-visco-sym}

For an isotropic material, the relaxation tensor decomposes as
\(\uuuu{C}(t,t') = 3k(t,t')\,\uuuu{J} + 2\mu(t,t')\,\uuuu{K}\). At the
discrete level the 6\((n+1)\)\$\times\(6\)(n+1)\$ block matrix inherits
this structure: its information is entirely captured by independent
scalar \((n+1)\times(n+1)\) \textbf{parameter blocks}, one per
independent modulus of the material symmetry class.

\textbf{\texttt{visco\_paramsym}} extracts these blocks from a block
matrix:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{params }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_paramsym(array, sym, theta}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, phi}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, psi}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{, unitsize}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{)}
\CommentTok{\# array        : block relaxation/creep matrix, size 6(n+1)×6(n+1)}
\CommentTok{\# sym          : material symmetry — ISO, TI, ORTHO, ANISO}
\CommentTok{\# theta,phi,psi: Euler rotation angles of the symmetry axes (default 0)}
\CommentTok{\# unitsize     : size of each tensor block (default 6)}
\CommentTok{\# returns      : list of (n+1)×(n+1) sub{-}matrices}
\CommentTok{\#                ISO   → [Vk, Vmu]                   (bulk, shear)}
\CommentTok{\#                TI    → [Vl, Vm, Vn, Vp, Vq]        (5 moduli)}
\CommentTok{\#                ORTHO → [V11,V22,V33,V44,V55,V66,V12,V13,V23]}
\CommentTok{\#                ANISO → 21 components}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\textbf{\texttt{visco\_tensor}} reconstructs the full block matrix from
the parameter blocks (inverse of \texttt{visco\_paramsym}):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{array }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_tensor(params, angles}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[])}
\CommentTok{\# params : list of (n+1)×(n+1) sub{-}matrices}
\CommentTok{\# angles : rotation angles matching those used in visco\_paramsym}
\CommentTok{\# returns: block matrix of size 6(n+1)×6(n+1)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Continuing the previous example (\texttt{vl}, \texttt{t3}):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{M\_relax }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ vl.relaxation\_mat(t3)}
\NormalTok{Vk, Vmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_paramsym(M\_relax, ISO)   }\CommentTok{\# bulk and shear (n+1)×(n+1) blocks}

\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Bulk relaxation block Vk  [k(ti, tj)]:"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(Vk)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{Shear relaxation block Vmu  [mu(ti, tj)]:"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(Vmu)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Bulk relaxation block Vk  [k(ti, tj)]:
[[ 1.666667  0.        0.        0.      ]
 [-0.357895  1.471521  0.        0.      ]
 [-0.015853 -0.616222  1.54099   0.      ]
 [-0.041673  0.074063 -0.884876  1.598983]]

Shear relaxation block Vmu  [mu(ti, tj)]:
[[ 0.833333  0.        0.        0.      ]
 [-0.173859  0.741481  0.        0.      ]
 [-0.005862 -0.288234  0.791704  0.      ]
 [-0.023549  0.018488 -0.420428  0.85231 ]]
\end{verbatim}

The \((i,j)\) entry of \texttt{Vk} (resp. \texttt{Vmu}) is the bulk
(resp. shear) relaxation modulus \(k(t_i,t_j)\) (resp.
\(\mu(t_i,t_j)\)). The lower-triangular structure reflects causality.
Reconstruction with \texttt{visco\_tensor} is exact:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{M\_check }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_tensor([Vk, Vmu], [])}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Max reconstruction error:"}\NormalTok{, }\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(M\_relax }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ M\_check).}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{())}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}
Max reconstruction error: 2.220446049250313e-16
\end{verbatim}

\section{ALV Eshelby problem and Hill tensor
kernel}\label{sec-ageing-eshelby}

\subsection{Problem statement}\label{problem-statement}

Consider an ellipsoidal inclusion \(\mathcal{E}\) (shape tensor
\(\uu{A}\)) embedded in an infinite ALV matrix with relaxation kernel
\(\uuuu{C}^0(t,t')\). The inclusion has relaxation kernel
\(\uuuu{C}^\mathcal{E}(t,t')\). Under remote strain loading
\(\uu{E}(t)\), the strain and stress fields inside \(\mathcal{E}\)
remain \textbf{uniform} --- a key result that extends the Eshelby
theorem to ALV (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJSS2016}{Barthélémy et al.,
2016}).

\subsection{ALV Hill tensor kernel}\label{alv-hill-tensor-kernel}

The strain inside the inclusion due to a uniform eigenstress
(polarization) \(\uu{p}(t)\) in \(\mathcal{E}\) is given by the
\textbf{ALV Hill tensor kernel} \(\uuuu{P}_\mathcal{E}(t,t')\):

\[
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{|\mathcal{E}}(t) = -\uuuu{P}_\mathcal{E}(t,\bullet) \dcirc \uu{p}(\bullet)
\]

The formula for \(\uuuu{P}_\mathcal{E}(t,t')\) and its derivation are
given in Appendix~\ref{sec-hill_alv} (\ref{eq-alv-hill-kernel}). The
fundamental result is the \textbf{block property}
(\ref{eq-block-hill-prop}): over a discrete time grid,

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-block-hill}{
\bigl[\mathbf{P}_\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{R}^0)\bigr]_{ij}
= \mathbb{P}_\mathcal{E}\!\bigl([\mathbf{R}^0]_{ij}\bigr)
}\end{equation}

Block \((i,j)\) of the block Hill matrix equals the \textbf{elastic Hill
tensor} evaluated at the \((i,j)\) block of the reference relaxation
matrix. This reduces ALV homogenization to a collection of elastic Hill
tensor evaluations.

\subsection{Isotropic matrix case}\label{isotropic-matrix-case}

When the matrix is isotropic,
\(\uuuu{C}^0(t,t') = 3k^0(t,t')\,\mathbb{J} + 2\mu^0(t,t')\,\mathbb{K}\),
the ALV Hill tensor kernel decouples (see Appendix~\ref{sec-hill_alv}):

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-hill-alv-iso}{
\uuuu{P}_\mathcal{E}(t,t') =
\volt{\!\left(k^0 + \tfrac{4}{3}\mu^0\right)}_{(t,t')}\,\uuuu{U}^{\uu{A}}
+ \volt{\mu^0}_{(t,t')}\,\bigl(\uuuu{V}^{\uu{A}} - \uuuu{U}^{\uu{A}}\bigr)
}\end{equation}

where \(\uuuu{U}^{\uu{A}}\) and \(\uuuu{V}^{\uu{A}}\) are the purely
geometric tensors defined in Appendix~\ref{sec-hill_elas}. The
block-\((i,j)\) form recovered from \ref{eq-block-hill} is:

\[
[\mathbf{P}_\mathcal{E}]_{ij} = \mathbb{P}_\mathcal{E}\!\left(3k^0_{ij}\,\mathbb{J}+2\mu^0_{ij}\,\mathbb{K}\right)
\]

For a \textbf{sphere}, \(\mathbb{P}_{sphere}(\mathbb{C})\) depends on
\(k\) and \(\mu\) only through the combination \(k+4\mu/3\) and \(\mu\),
giving explicit scalar formulas (see Appendix~\ref{sec-hill_elas}). For
general spheroids, the formulas involve elliptic integrals of the aspect
ratio (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJES2019}{Barthélémy et al., 2019},
Appendix A).

\subsection{Strain concentration and contribution
tensors}\label{strain-concentration-and-contribution-tensors}

For an inclusion \(\mathcal{E}\) with stiffness kernel
\(\uuuu{C}^\mathcal{E}\) in a reference medium with kernel
\(\uuuu{C}^0\), the \textbf{strain concentration tensor} (dilute Eshelby
solution) satisfies:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-alv-concentration}{
\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}_{|\mathcal{E}} = \uuuu{a}^\mathcal{E} \dcirc \uu{E},
\qquad
\uuuu{a}^\mathcal{E} = \volt{\!\bigl(H\,\uuuu{I} + \uuuu{P}_\mathcal{E} \dcirc (\uuuu{C}^\mathcal{E} - \uuuu{C}^0)\bigr)}
}\end{equation}

In block matrix form:
\(\mathbf{A}^\mathcal{E} = \bigl[\mathbf{I} + \mathbf{P}_\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{R}^0)\cdot(\mathbf{R}^\mathcal{E} - \mathbf{R}^0)\bigr]^{-1}\).

The \textbf{strain contribution tensor} (relating the far-field
disturbance to the macroscopic strain) is:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-alv-contribution}{
\uuuu{N}^\mathcal{E} = (\uuuu{C}^\mathcal{E} - \uuuu{C}^0) \dcirc \uuuu{a}^\mathcal{E}
= \volt{\!\bigl(\uuuu{P}_\mathcal{E} + \volt{(\uuuu{C}^\mathcal{E} - \uuuu{C}^0)}\bigr)}
}\end{equation}

These Volterra-kernel expressions are the exact analogues of the elastic
concentration and contribution tensors with matrix inverses replaced by
Volterra inverses.

\section{\texorpdfstring{\emph{Echoes} commands for ALV
homogenization}{Echoes commands for ALV homogenization}}\label{sec-visco-echoes}

\subsection{RVE setup}\label{rve-setup}

Phases are defined with a \texttt{visco\_prop} argument providing the
creep or relaxation kernel as a function of two time arguments:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ver }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ver[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                          prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cs\},}
\NormalTok{                          visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: (Js, CREEP)\})}
\NormalTok{ver[}\StringTok{"INCL"}\NormalTok{]  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(omega), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                          prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Ci\},}
\NormalTok{                          visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: (Ji, CREEP)\},}
\NormalTok{                          fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{f)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The reference medium relaxation matrix is set on the RVE:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ver.set\_visco\_mat(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, visco\_law(Js, CREEP).relaxation\_mat(T))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

where \texttt{T} is the time discretization array. This builds the block
relaxation matrix \(\mathbf{R}^0\) for the reference medium.

\subsection{Time-domain
homogenization}\label{time-domain-homogenization}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{V }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize\_visco(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ver, time\_series}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{T,}
\NormalTok{                     scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT,      }\CommentTok{\# or SC, DIFF, DIL, MAX}
\NormalTok{                     epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The output \texttt{V} is the effective block relaxation matrix of size
\(6(n+1)\times 6(n+1)\). Its inverse gives the effective creep matrix;
the uniaxial creep compliance for loading at \(t_0\) is:
\[J^{hom}(t_i,t_0) = [\mathbf{V}^{-1}]_{6i,\,0}\]

\subsection{Other inclusion types}\label{other-inclusion-types}

For \textbf{cracks} (penny-shaped inclusions) and \textbf{n-layer
spheres} (coated inclusions), the same \texttt{homogenize\_visco}
interface applies; see Chapter~\ref{sec-crack-object} and
Chapter~\ref{sec-nlayer_sphere} for the respective \emph{Echoes}
commands.

For \textbf{cracks}, the viscoelastic compliance is obtained by taking
the limit of a flat spheroid, analogously to the elastic case. The
viscoelastic kernel is supplied via \texttt{visco\_prop}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ver[}\StringTok{"CRK"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ crack(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{penny\_shaped(omega), density}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{rho,}
\NormalTok{                   visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: (Jc, CREEP)\})}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

For \textbf{n-layer spheres}, the computation does not rely on a Hill
tensor. Instead, it extends the elastic result of
(\citeproc{ref-herve1993}{Hervé and Zaoui, 1993}) to the ageing linear
viscoelastic setting using the block structure of the Volterra
operators, with possible \textbf{viscoelastic interfaces} generalising
the elastic imperfect-interface framework of
(\citeproc{ref-herve-luanco2014}{Hervé-Luanco, 2014}). Kernels are
supplied layer-by-layer (list of \texttt{(func,\ type)} tuples) for
\texttt{visco\_prop}, and interface-by-interface for
\texttt{interf\_visco\_prop}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{ver[}\StringTok{"SPH"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sphere\_nlayers(nb\_layers}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, radius}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{R, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{f,}
\NormalTok{                             visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: [(J1, CREEP), (J2, CREEP)]\},}
\NormalTok{                             interf\_visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: [(Ki, CREEP)]\})}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Homogenization schemes}\label{sec-visco-schemes}

For a matrix composite with matrix stiffness kernel \(\uuuu{C}^0\) and
inclusion sets \(r\) (volume fractions \(\varphi_r\), stiffness kernels
\(\uuuu{C}^r\)), the general formula for the effective stiffness kernel
is:

\[
\uuuu{C}^{hom} = \uuuu{C}^0 + \sum_r \varphi_r\,(\uuuu{C}^r - \uuuu{C}^0) \dcirc \langle\uuuu{a}^r\rangle
\]

Different schemes correspond to different choices of how
\(\langle\uuuu{a}^r\rangle\) is estimated.

\subsection{Maxwell scheme}\label{maxwell-scheme}

The \textbf{Maxwell} (or Ponte Castañeda-Willis) scheme equates the
far-field disturbance of all inclusions to that of an equivalent
homogeneous particle of shape \(\Omega\) (distribution shape):

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-maxwell-alv}{
\volt{\uuuu{C}^{MX}} = \volt{\uuuu{C}^0} + \volt{\!\left(\volt{\!\left(\sum_r \varphi_r\,\uuuu{N}^r\right)} - \uuuu{P}_\Omega\right)}
}\end{equation}

In block matrix form:
\((\mathbf{R}^{MX})^{-1} = (\mathbf{R}^0)^{-1} + \bigl[(\sum_r\varphi_r\mathbf{N}^r)^{-1} - \mathbf{P}_\Omega\bigr]^{-1}\).
This scheme is equivalent to the elastic PCW scheme and provides a
rigorous bound structure (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJES2019}{Barthélémy
et al., 2019}).

\subsection{Dilute scheme (DIL)}\label{dilute-scheme-dil}

The \textbf{dilute} (DIL) scheme --- also known as the non-interaction
approximation (NIA, (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJES2019}{Barthélémy et
al., 2019})) in its creep compliance form --- treats each inclusion
independently in the matrix:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-dil-alv}{
\uuuu{C}^{DIL} = \uuuu{C}^0 + \sum_r \varphi_r\,\uuuu{N}^r
}\end{equation}

This is the first-order estimate, valid for small volume fractions.

\subsection{Mori-Tanaka (MT) scheme}\label{mori-tanaka-mt-scheme}

The \textbf{MT} scheme estimates the strain concentration using
auxiliary Eshelby problems where the remote strain equals the matrix
average \(\langle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}\rangle_{matrix}\), combined
with the consistency rule
\(\uu{E}(t) = \langle\boldsymbol{\varepsilon}(t)\rangle\). This gives
(\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJES2019}{Barthélémy et al., 2019}):

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-mtb-alv}{
\uuuu{C}^{MT} = \uuuu{C}^0 +
\sum_r \varphi_r\,\uuuu{N}^r \dcirc
\volt{\!\left((1-\textstyle\sum_s\varphi_s)\,H\,\uuuu{I} + \sum_s \varphi_s\,\uuuu{a}^s\right)}
}\end{equation}

When all inclusions have the same shape (single set), the MT scheme
reduces to the standard \textbf{Mori-Tanaka} scheme with matrix as
reference:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-MT-block}{
\mathbf{R}^{MT} = \Bigl(\sum_r f_r\,\mathbf{R}^r\cdot\mathbf{A}^r_{MT}\Bigr)\cdot\Bigl(\sum_r f_r\,\mathbf{A}^r_{MT}\Bigr)^{-1}
}\end{equation}

where
\(\mathbf{A}^r_{MT} = \bigl[\mathbf{I} + \mathbf{P}_{\mathcal{E}^r}(\mathbf{R}^m)\cdot(\mathbf{R}^r - \mathbf{R}^m)\bigr]^{-1}\)
and the reference is the matrix block stiffness \(\mathbf{R}^m\).

\subsection{Self-consistent scheme and differential
scheme}\label{self-consistent-scheme-and-differential-scheme}

In the \textbf{self-consistent} (SC) scheme the reference medium is the
unknown effective medium: \(\mathbf{R}^0 = \mathbf{R}^{SC}\), solved
iteratively. The \textbf{differential} (DIFF) scheme builds the
effective medium by progressively introducing inclusions in
infinitesimal increments. Both are available in \emph{Echoes} via the
\texttt{scheme} argument.

\section{Validation: non-ageing PMMA
composite}\label{sec-laplace-validation}

For a \textbf{non-ageing} PMMA matrix the Rabotnov class is stable under
homogenization: the effective shear modulus under the MAX and DIL
schemes is again of Rabotnov type with analytically computable
parameters (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJSS2016}{Barthélémy et al., 2016};
\citeproc{ref-sevostianov2015}{Sevostianov et al., 2015}). These
closed-form expressions validate the ALV time-domain algorithm for both
\textbf{rigid inclusions} and \textbf{spherical pores}. The matrix shear
modulus follows the \textbf{fraction-exponential} (Rabotnov) law

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-rabotnov}{
\mu(t) = \mu^0\!\left(1 + \lambda\,I_\alpha(\beta,t)\right),
\qquad
I_\alpha(\beta,t) = \frac{1}{\beta}\!\left(1 - E_{1+\alpha}(-\beta\,t^{1+\alpha})\right)
}\end{equation}

where \(E_a\) is the \textbf{one-parameter Mittag-Leffler function},
defined by the entire series

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-ml-def}{
E_a(z) = \sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^k}{\Gamma(ak+1)}, \qquad a>0,
}\end{equation}

which generalizes the exponential (\(E_1(z)=e^z\)). The function
\(I_\alpha(\beta,t)\) in \ref{eq-rabotnov} is the primitive of the
\textbf{Rabotnov function} \(\mathfrak{R}_\alpha\):

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-rabotnov-primitive}{
\mathfrak{R}_\alpha(\beta,t)
  = t^{\alpha}\,E_{1+\alpha,\,1+\alpha}\!\left(-\beta\,t^{1+\alpha}\right),
\qquad
I_\alpha(\beta,t)
  = \int_0^t \mathfrak{R}_\alpha(\beta,s)\,\ud s
  = \frac{1}{\beta}\!\left(1 - E_{1+\alpha}\!\left(-\beta\,t^{1+\alpha}\right)\right),
}\end{equation}

where \(E_{a,b}(z)=\sum_{k=0}^\infty z^k/\Gamma(ak+b)\) is the
two-parameter Mittag-Leffler function. For \(\alpha\in(-1,0)\) and
\(\beta,\lambda>0\) the relaxation modulus \(\mu(t)\) decreases
monotonically from \(\mu^0\) toward the long-time value
\(\mu^0(1+\lambda/\beta)\), with the Rabotnov exponent \(\alpha\)
controlling the rate. The evaluation of \(E_a\) relies on the
\href{https://github.com/khinsen/mittag-leffler}{\texttt{mittag-leffler}}
package (\citeproc{ref-hinsen2025}{Hinsen, 2025}).

The bulk modulus is elastic (\(k=k^0\), constant). The LC transform of
\ref{eq-rabotnov} is:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-rabotnov-lc}{
\tilde{\mu}(p) = \mu^0\!\left(1 + \frac{\lambda}{p^{1+\alpha} + \beta}\right)
}\end{equation}

Parameters (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJSS2016}{Barthélémy et al., 2016}):
\(k^0=5.97\) GPa, \(\mu^0=1.7\) GPa, \(\alpha=-0.46\), \(\beta=0.98\)
h\(^{-(1+\alpha)}\), \(\lambda=-0.495\) h\(^{-(1+\alpha)}\). Rigid
inclusions: \(C_i=10^6\,\mathbb{C}(k^0,\mu^0)\); spherical pores:
\(C_i=10^{-10}\,\mathbb{C}(k^0,\mu^0)\).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{k0, mu0 }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{5.97}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.7}
\NormalTok{alpha0, beta0, lambda0 }\OperatorTok{=} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.46}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.98}\NormalTok{, }\OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{0.495}

\NormalTok{Cmat }\OperatorTok{=} \KeywordTok{lambda}\NormalTok{ t, tp: }\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{J4 }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ lambda0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{I\_Rabotnov(t}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{tp, alpha0, beta0))}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{K4}
\NormalTok{Cinc }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e6}   \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ stiff\_kmu(k0, mu0)   }\CommentTok{\# rigid spheres}
\NormalTok{Cpor }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e{-}10} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ stiff\_kmu(k0, mu0)   }\CommentTok{\# spherical pores}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ Chom\_rig(T, sch, f):}
\NormalTok{    ver }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"MAT"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ver[}\StringTok{"MAT"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                            visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: (Cmat, RELAXATION)\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{f)}
\NormalTok{    ver[}\StringTok{"INC"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                            prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cinc\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{f)}
\NormalTok{    V }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize\_visco(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ver, time\_series}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{T,}
\NormalTok{                         scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \_, Vmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_paramsym(V, ISO)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Vmu   }\CommentTok{\# relaxation matrix}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ Chom\_por(T, sch, f):}
\NormalTok{    ver }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"MAT"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ver[}\StringTok{"MAT"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                            visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: (Cmat, RELAXATION)\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{f)}
\NormalTok{    ver[}\StringTok{"INC"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical, symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                            prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: Cpor\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{f)}
\NormalTok{    V }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize\_visco(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ver, time\_series}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{T,}
\NormalTok{                         scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \_, Vmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_paramsym(V, ISO)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ Vmu   }\CommentTok{\# relaxation matrix}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ Lmu\_sph\_rig\_ML\_DIL(t, f):}
\NormalTok{    mu0d  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mu0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{6}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0))}
\NormalTok{    a0mu  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ lambda0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0d)}
\NormalTok{    a1mu  }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f))}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0))}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{a0mu}
\NormalTok{    beta1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ beta0 }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{lambda0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}
\NormalTok{    B }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ beta0}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{a0mu}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{beta1}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{a1mu}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ a0mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{beta1}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{a1mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{beta0}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{beta0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{beta1}
\NormalTok{    sqD }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sqrt(B}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{C)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  beta3 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (B}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{sqD)}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  beta4 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (B}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{sqD)}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{    a3mu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (beta0}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{beta3)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(beta3}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{beta1)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(beta3}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{beta4)}
\NormalTok{    a4mu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (beta0}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{beta4)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(beta4}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{beta1)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(beta4}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{beta3)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{mu0d}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ a3mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{I\_Rabotnov(t,alpha0,beta3) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ a4mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{I\_Rabotnov(t,alpha0,beta4))}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ mu\_sph\_rig\_ML\_DIL(t, f):}
\NormalTok{    mu0d  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mu0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{6}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0))}
\NormalTok{    a0mu  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ lambda0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0d)}
\NormalTok{    a1mu  }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f))}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0))}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{a0mu}
\NormalTok{    beta1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ beta0 }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{lambda0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ mu0d}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ a0mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{I\_Rabotnov(t,alpha0,beta0) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ a1mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{I\_Rabotnov(t,alpha0,beta1))}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ Lmu\_sph\_rig\_ML\_MAX(t, f):}
\NormalTok{    mu0MX }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ mu0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{6}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{8}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0))}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{6}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{f)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0))}
\NormalTok{    b0mu  }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{3}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{lambda0}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{6}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{8}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0))}
\NormalTok{    b1mu  }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{5}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{lambda0}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{6}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{f}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{9}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{8}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0))}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}
\NormalTok{    beta1 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ beta0 }\OperatorTok{+} \DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{lambda0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(k0}\OperatorTok{+}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{mu0)}
\NormalTok{    B }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ beta0}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{b0mu}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{beta1}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{b1mu}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ b0mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{beta1}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{b1mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{beta0}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{beta0}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{beta1}
\NormalTok{    sqD }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ sqrt(B}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{4}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{C)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  delta3 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (B}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{sqD)}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  delta4 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (B}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{sqD)}\OperatorTok{/}\DecValTok{2}
\NormalTok{    b3mu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (beta0}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{delta3)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(delta3}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{beta1)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(delta3}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{delta4)}
\NormalTok{    b4mu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (beta0}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{delta4)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(delta4}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{beta1)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{(delta4}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{delta3)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return} \FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{mu0MX}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\DecValTok{1} \OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b3mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{I\_Rabotnov(t,alpha0,delta3) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ b4mu}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{I\_Rabotnov(t,alpha0,delta4))}

\NormalTok{        (ax\_Lr, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Rigid spheres — shear creep\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,        }\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{L}\DecValTok{\^{}\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{t}\KeywordTok{)}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{ }\PreprocessorTok{[GPa$\^{}\{{-}1\}$]}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{        (ax\_mr, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Rigid spheres — shear relaxation\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,   }\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{$\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{t}\KeywordTok{)}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{ }\PreprocessorTok{[GPa]}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{        (ax\_Lp, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Spherical pores — shear creep\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,      }\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{L}\DecValTok{\^{}\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{t}\KeywordTok{)}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{ }\PreprocessorTok{[GPa$\^{}\{{-}1\}$]}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{),}
\NormalTok{        (ax\_mp, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Spherical pores — shear relaxation\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{$\textbackslash{}m}\VerbatimStringTok{u}\KeywordTok{(}\VerbatimStringTok{t}\KeywordTok{)}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{ }\PreprocessorTok{[GPa]}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)]:}
\NormalTok{    ax.set\_xlabel(}\VerbatimStringTok{r\textquotesingle{}}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{t}\DecValTok{$}\VerbatimStringTok{ }\PreprocessorTok{[h]}\VerbatimStringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  ax.set\_ylabel(ylbl)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  ax.set\_title(ttl)}
\NormalTok{    ax.grid(}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}\OperatorTok{;}\NormalTok{  ax.legend(fontsize}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{)}

Crosses (Echoes time-domain) coincide with the lines (closed-form ML
solutions), confirming that the ALV algorithm correctly reproduces the
Rabotnov homogenization formulas for both rigid inclusions and porous
media.

\section{Application: Granger ageing creep
law}\label{sec-ageing-application}

We apply the framework to an \textbf{ageing matrix--inclusion composite}
with the creep law of (\citeproc{ref-barthelemyIJES2019}{Barthélémy et
al., 2019}), adapted from the Granger model. Both phases obey a
compliance of the form

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-granger-law}{
\uuuu{J}(t,t') = \left(\frac{1}{E} + f_a(t')\sum_{i=1}^{2}s_i\!\left(1-e^{-(t-t')/\tau_i}\right)\right)\mathbb{T}_\nu,
\quad
\mathbb{T}_\nu = (1-2\nu)\,\mathbb{J} + (1+\nu)\,\mathbb{K},
\quad f_a(t')=e^{-(t'/\tau_a)^2}
}\end{equation}

The ageing factor \(f_a(t')\) decreases with loading age \(t'\),
reflecting stiffening of the material over time. Parameters
(dimensionless):

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}lccccccc@{}}
\toprule\noalign{}
Phase & \(\nu\) & \(E\) & \(s_1\) & \(s_2\) & \(\tau_1\) & \(\tau_2\) &
\(\tau_a\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
Phase & \(\nu\) & \(E\) & \(s_1\) & \(s_2\) & \(\tau_1\) & \(\tau_2\) &
\(\tau_a\) \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\tabularnewline
\caption{Granger-type law parameters for matrix and
inclusions.}\label{tbl-granger}\tabularnewline
\endlastfoot
Matrix & 0.25 & 1 & 2 & 3 & 2 & 10 & 30 \\
Inclusion & 0.15 & 10 & 0.5 & 0.7 & 0.1 & 7 & 15 \\
\end{longtable}

The RVE consists of oblate spheroidal inclusions (\(\omega=0.1\))
distributed isotropically in the matrix (Fig.~\ref{fig-rve-visco}).

\begin{figure}

\centering{

\includegraphics[width=2.08333in,height=\textheight,keepaspectratio]{index_files/mediabag/6_viscoelasticity/../img/ver2_visco.pdf}

}

\caption{\label{fig-rve-visco}RVE: matrix (grey) with oblate spheroidal
inclusions (\(\omega=0.1\), blue).}

\end{figure}%

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ Js\_Kelvin2(nu, E, s1, s2, tau1, tau2, tau\_a):}
    \CommentTok{"""Ageing creep compliance: @eq{-}granger{-}law."""}
    \KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ f(t, tp):}
\NormalTok{        fa }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ exp(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(tp}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{tau\_a)}\OperatorTok{**}\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{        je }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{E }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ fa}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(s1}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{exp(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(t}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{tp)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{tau1)) }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ s2}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{(}\FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{exp(}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{(t}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{tp)}\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{tau2)))}
        \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ je}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{((}\FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{2.}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{nu)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{J4 }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{1.}\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{nu)}\OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{K4)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ f}

\NormalTok{Js\_m }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Js\_Kelvin2(}\FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{,  }\FloatTok{2.}\NormalTok{,   }\FloatTok{3.}\NormalTok{,   }\FloatTok{2.}\NormalTok{,   }\FloatTok{10.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{30.}\NormalTok{)   }\CommentTok{\# matrix}
\NormalTok{Js\_i }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ Js\_Kelvin2(}\FloatTok{0.15}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{10.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{,  }\FloatTok{0.7}\NormalTok{,  }\FloatTok{0.1}\NormalTok{,  }\FloatTok{7.}\NormalTok{,  }\FloatTok{15.}\NormalTok{)  }\CommentTok{\# inclusion}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ Chom\_G(omega, T, sch, f):}
    \CommentTok{"""Effective shear creep compliance matrix (block lower{-}triangular)."""}
\NormalTok{    ver\_g }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"MAT"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    ver\_g[}\StringTok{"MAT"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spherical,      symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                              visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: (Js\_m, CREEP)\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{f)}
\NormalTok{    ver\_g[}\StringTok{"INC"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(omega), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                              visco\_prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: (Js\_i, CREEP)\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{f)}
\NormalTok{    V }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize\_visco(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ver\_g, time\_series}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{T,}
\NormalTok{                         scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{sch, epsrel}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, maxnb}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{100}\NormalTok{, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{    \_, Vmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_paramsym(V, ISO)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ linalg.inv(Vmu)}

\KeywordTok{def}\NormalTok{ Cvisco\_G(js, T):}
    \CommentTok{"""Single{-}phase shear creep compliance matrix."""}
\NormalTok{    \_, Vmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ visco\_paramsym(visco\_law(js, CREEP).relaxation\_mat(T), ISO)}
    \ControlFlowTok{return}\NormalTok{ linalg.inv(Vmu)}

\NormalTok{n\_app }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{200}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\chapter{Derivatives of effective properties}\label{sec-derivatives}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-important-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-important-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Objectives}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

This tutorial presents the \texttt{homogenize\_derivative} function for
computing the derivatives of the effective stiffness tensor with respect
to the moduli of individual phases. These derivatives give access to
\textbf{second-order moments} (quadratic averages) of the local strain
and stress fields, which are central to nonlinear extensions (see
Chapter~\ref{sec-nonlinear}).

\end{tcolorbox}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, arc=.35mm, bottomrule=.15mm, bottomtitle=1mm, breakable, colback=white, colbacktitle=quarto-callout-tip-color!10!white, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, coltitle=black, left=2mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, opacitybacktitle=0.6, rightrule=.15mm, title={Imports}, titlerule=0mm, toprule=.15mm, toptitle=1mm]

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ numpy }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ np}
\ImportTok{from}\NormalTok{ echoes }\ImportTok{import} \OperatorTok{*}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ math}
\ImportTok{import}\NormalTok{ matplotlib.pyplot }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ plt}

\NormalTok{np.set\_printoptions(precision}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, suppress}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}

\end{Shaded}

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Second-order moments of local fields}\label{sec-second-moments}

A key result of micromechanics (\citeproc{ref-kreher1990}{Kreher, 1990})
is that the \textbf{second-order moments} (quadratic averages) of the
local strain field in a phase \(r\) can be obtained directly from the
derivative of the effective stiffness with respect to the elastic moduli
of that phase. For an isotropic phase \(r\) with shear modulus
\(\mu_r\), the mean deviatoric strain variance in phase \(r\) reads:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-kreher-iso}{
f_r\,\langle\varepsilon_d^2\rangle_{\Omega_r} = \frac{1}{2}\,\uu{E}:\frac{\partial\uuuu{C}^{\rm hom}}{\partial\mu_r}:\uu{E}
}\end{equation}

where \(f_r\) is the volume fraction of phase \(r\), \(\uu{E}\) the
macroscopic strain, and
\(\varepsilon_d^2 = \uu{\varepsilon}':\uu{\varepsilon}'\) (with
\(\uu{\varepsilon}' = \uu{\varepsilon} - \frac{1}{3}\mathrm{tr}(\uu{\varepsilon})\,\uu{1}\)
the deviatoric strain).

More generally, the full second-order strain moment tensor in phase
\(r\) is related to the derivative with respect to the full stiffness
tensor:

\begin{equation}\protect\phantomsection\label{eq-kreher-aniso}{
f_r\,\langle\varepsilon_{ij}\,\varepsilon_{kl}\rangle_{\Omega_r} = \frac{1}{2}\,E_{mn}\,\frac{\partial C^{\rm hom}_{mnpq}}{\partial c^r_{ijkl}}\,E_{pq}
}\end{equation}

where \texttt{index} in \texttt{homogenize\_derivative} addresses
specific components \(c^r_{ijkl}\) in the condensed representation.
These relations underpin the \textbf{modified secant method} for
nonlinear composites (\citeproc{ref-suquet1997}{Suquet, 1997}) (see
Chapter~\ref{sec-nonlinear}).

\section{\texorpdfstring{The \texttt{homogenize\_derivative}
function}{The homogenize\_derivative function}}\label{sec-homogenize-derivative}

After computing the effective properties of a RVE with
\texttt{homogenize}, the derivative of the effective stiffness with
respect to a phase modulus is obtained by:

\begin{verbatim}
dChom = homogenize_derivative(prop="C", rve=myrve, scheme=sch,
                               phase="PHASE_NAME", index=i, sym=ISO)
\end{verbatim}

where:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  \texttt{phase} is the name of the phase whose modulus is
  differentiated
\item
  \texttt{index} is the index of the parameter in the condensed
  representation (0 for the first parameter, 1 for the second, etc.).
  For isotropic tensors (\texttt{ISO}): index 0 corresponds to \(3k\)
  and index 1 to \(2\mu\)
\item
  \texttt{sym} is the symmetry class (\texttt{ISO}, \texttt{TI},
  \texttt{ORTHO}, \texttt{ANISO})
\end{itemize}

For n-layer spheres, an additional \texttt{layer} argument specifies
which layer:

\begin{verbatim}
dChom = homogenize_derivative(prop="C", rve=myrve, scheme=sch,
                               phase="SPN", layer=0, index=1, sym=ISO)
\end{verbatim}

\section{Example: sensitivity to matrix shear
modulus}\label{example-sensitivity-to-matrix-shear-modulus}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{myrve }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ rve(matrix}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(}\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                           prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{32.}\NormalTok{)\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.8}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"PORE"}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ ellipsoid(shape}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{spheroidal(}\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{), symmetrize}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{[ISO],}
\NormalTok{                          prop}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{\{}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{: stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{1.e{-}6}\NormalTok{)\}, fraction}\OperatorTok{=}\FloatTok{0.2}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}

\section{Validation by finite
differences}\label{validation-by-finite-differences}

The analytical derivative can be validated against a finite-difference
approximation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{mu\_s }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{32.}
\NormalTok{eps }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.e{-}5}

\CommentTok{\# Perturbed computations}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].set\_prop(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, mu\_s }\OperatorTok{+}\NormalTok{ eps))}
\NormalTok{Cp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}

\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].set\_prop(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, mu\_s }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ eps))}
\NormalTok{Cm }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Restore original}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].set\_prop(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, mu\_s))}

\NormalTok{dC\_fd }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ (Cp.array }\OperatorTok{{-}}\NormalTok{ Cm.array) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ eps)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"Finite difference dChom/dmu:}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{, dC\_fd)}

\CommentTok{\# Compare with analytical (index=1 gives d/d(2mu), so multiply by 2)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{Analytical dChom/d(2mu) * 2:}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{"}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dC\_dmu.array)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{"}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{Max difference:"}\NormalTok{, np.}\BuiltInTok{max}\NormalTok{(np.}\BuiltInTok{abs}\NormalTok{(dC\_fd }\OperatorTok{{-}} \DecValTok{2} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dC\_dmu.array)))}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

 [-0.06650552  1.21695657 -0.06650552  0.          0.          0.        ]
 [-0.06650552 -0.06650552  1.21695657  0.          0.          0.        ]

 [-0.06650552  1.21695657 -0.06650552  0.          0.          0.        ]
 [-0.06650552 -0.06650552  1.21695657  0.          0.          0.        ]

\section{Second-order strain moments in
practice}\label{second-order-strain-moments-in-practice}

Using (\ref{eq-kreher-iso}), the mean deviatoric strain variance in the
solid phase can be computed from the derivative with respect to
\(\mu_s\). For a macroscopic strain
\(\uu{E} = E\,\uv{e}_1 \otimes \uv{e}_1\) (uniaxial):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Restore the RVE from the previous example}
\NormalTok{myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].set\_prop(}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, stiff\_kmu(}\FloatTok{72.}\NormalTok{, }\FloatTok{32.}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{C }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT, verbose}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{False}\NormalTok{)}

\CommentTok{\# Derivative w.r.t. mu\_s  (index=1 gives d/d(2*mu))}
\NormalTok{dC\_dmu }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ homogenize\_derivative(prop}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"C"}\NormalTok{, rve}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{myrve, scheme}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{MT,}
\NormalTok{                                phase}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{, index}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, sym}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ISO)}

\CommentTok{\# Macroscopic strain: uniaxial E\_11 = 1, all other = 0}
\NormalTok{E }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ np.zeros((}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{))}
\NormalTok{E[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \FloatTok{1.}   \CommentTok{\# E\_11 in Kelvin{-}Mandel (index 0)}

\CommentTok{\# f\_s * \textless{}eps\_d\^{}2\textgreater{}\_solid = (1/2) E : dChom/dmu\_s : E}
\CommentTok{\# Note: index=1 → d/d(2mu), so dChom/dmu = 2 * dC\_dmu}
\NormalTok{f\_s }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ myrve[}\StringTok{"SOLID"}\NormalTok{].fraction}
\NormalTok{eps\_d2\_solid }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{float}\NormalTok{(E.T }\OperatorTok{@}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{2.} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ dC\_dmu.array) }\OperatorTok{@}\NormalTok{ E) }\OperatorTok{/}\NormalTok{ (}\FloatTok{2.} \OperatorTok{*}\NormalTok{ f\_s)}

\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"f\_s = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{f\_s}\SpecialCharTok{:.2f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\SpecialStringTok{f"\textless{}eps\_d\^{}2\textgreater{}\_solid (uniaxial E\_11=1) = }\SpecialCharTok{\{}\NormalTok{eps\_d2\_solid}\SpecialCharTok{:.6f\}}\SpecialStringTok{"}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\begin{verbatim}

<eps_d^2>_solid (uniaxial E_11=1) = 0.760598
\end{verbatim}

\(\,\)
